Zackary Mark
Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR


Sun Prairie's license plate reader cameras, from Flock to the proposed Axon
system: the community report, the police department's answers, contracts, costs,
and the letters, meetings and emails behind them.

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY




 Executive Summary
 Zackary Mark is a student advocate at Sun Prairie West High School. He runs two
 independent projects: SPASD Student Voice (since January 2026), which brings student
 input to Sun Prairie Area School District leaders, and Sun Prairie ALPR (since July 2026), a
 plain-language community report on the police department's license plate reader cameras.
 What changed because students spoke up
  • District newsletters will go to every student, confirmed in writing on October 21.
  • Students sat on principal interviews and met the superintendent finalists, and a Student
    Senate restarted at Sun Prairie West.
  • Students helped write the weighted grades plan; it went to the Board on November 9, with
    a final vote expected late November.
  • Superintendent Dr. Reed backed a District-Wide Student Council ("I love this idea"); Patrick
    Marsh signed on and other schools are responding.
  • The police chief put plate reader commitments in writing: a 14-day retention trial, no
    livestreaming, monthly audits and a public data-sharing agreement after signing.
 Also answered in writing: the district confirmed on October 1 that LGBTQIA+ protections
 are unchanged.
 What's still open: a formal path and sponsor for the student council, the final weighted
 grades vote, the city's Nov 17 budget vote on the Axon cameras, a public records request,
 and questions to the Board about the superintendent's license.
 How this record works: every email appears as a numbered card (E-001 onward) with
 sender, recipients and date. Officials' statements are quoted, not paraphrased, and labeled
 as statements rather than policy. Student names other than Zackary's are removed. Sources
 and an index are at the back.




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                             ↑ Contents      Page 5
WHAT HAPPENED, WHY I STARTED & WHY IT MATTERS




 What Happened, Why I Started & Why It
 Matters
 How this started, where things stand, and the words you need to read the rest.


 IN THIS PART
 At a Glance: Where Things Stand                                                            p. 8
 Glossary                                                                                   p. 9

 What happened
 Two cases involving former Sun Prairie West High School staff shaped how a lot of students
 feel about safety and trust in our district. These are the facts as reported in public court
 records and the news:
  • Robert Gilkey-Meisegeier, a former coach and dean of students, pleaded guilty in federal
    court to producing child sexual abuse material. On April 28, 2026, he was sentenced to 18
    years in federal prison. A related state case was dismissed after the federal conviction.
  • Jennifer Ploeger, a former principal, was charged with three misdemeanor counts of failing
    to report suspected child abuse. On June 25, 2026, she pleaded no contest to one count,
    the other two were dismissed, and she was placed in a deferred prosecution program. A no
    contest plea is not an admission of guilt, and she has not been convicted.
 Students mostly learned about both cases through rumors and the news, not from the
 district. That gap is a big reason this work exists.
 Sources: WKOW, Apr 28, 2026 · WTMJ, Jun 1, 2026 · WMTV15, Jun 25, 2026 · Spectrum News,
 Jun 25, 2026. Status as of October 20, 2026.

 Why I started
 In January 2026, I wrote to the Sun Prairie School Board after a hard stretch in our district left
 a lot of students unsure what happened and who to trust. I proposed a six-part Student
 Safety & Trust Framework: clearer communication after serious incidents, a plain explanation
 of how reporting works, the same rules in every building, a real student voice, better mental
 health support, and regular check-ins on how it's going.
 That letter turned into meetings with district leaders, a new Student Senate at a district high
 school, students helping choose a principal and a superintendent, a seat on the weighted
 grades work group, and a proposal for a district-wide Student Advisory Council. Along the
 way, the same questions about transparency led to meetings with the Sun Prairie Police
 Department about its license plate reader cameras.
 The goal hasn't changed. Student voice should be a regular, documented part of how SPASD
 makes decisions, not something that depends on one student or one principal.




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                               ↑ Contents          Page 6
WHAT HAPPENED, WHY I STARTED & WHY IT MATTERS




 Why it's important
 Students are the people most affected by what happens in our schools, but we're usually the
 last to hear about it and the least likely to be asked. When something serious happens and
 nobody tells us anything, rumors fill the gap. That makes it harder to trust the adults who are
 supposed to keep us safe, and harder to speak up when something is wrong.
 In outreach this year, most students said they feel physically safe at school. The problem is
 follow-through. As one student put it: "Yes I feel safe at school. Yes I would feel comfortable
 telling an adult. No adults do not listen."
 Decisions about grades, safety, communication, and leadership shape the next four years of
 our lives. Weighted grades and Act 95 decide who gets guaranteed admission to UW schools.
 How incidents are communicated decides whether we hear the truth or a rumor. Who leads
 our schools decides how all of that gets handled. Students should have a say before those
 decisions are final, not find out after.
 It also works when it's done right. Students served on principal and superintendent interview
 panels, helped restart a Student Senate, shaped the weighted grades plan, and got the
 district to start sending its newsletters to every student. Those changes happened because
 students asked, followed up, and kept a record.
 The same goes beyond school. The license plate reader cameras affect everyone who drives
 in Sun Prairie, including students and their families. People deserve to know what's being
 collected, who can see it, and how long it's kept, in plain language.

 Why this record exists
 I keep every email, invite, and set of notes so the conversations, answers, and commitments
 don't get lost, and so anyone can check what was said and when. Student names have been
 removed, except mine. This is an independent, student-led record, not an official Sun Prairie
 Area School District document, and it is shared in good faith, not to accuse any person.




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                              ↑ Contents     Page 7
WHAT HAPPENED, WHY I STARTED & WHY IT MATTERS




 At a Glance: Where Things Stand

 Where things stand (November 10, 2026)
  •    WON newsletters to every student. Asked for at the Oct 20 meeting; the district
      confirmed in writing on Oct 21 that newsletters will go to students starting in November.
  •   ANSWERED IN WRITING LGBTQIA+ protections unchanged (Oct 1).
  •    WON students in the room for leadership hiring. Students sat on the SPWHS
      principal interviews and met the superintendent finalists (April).
  •   WON a Student Senate restart at Sun Prairie West, with students elected in April.
  •    MOVING weighted grades. Students helped write the plan and four of them previewed
      it with Board members on Nov 2. Next: student rehearsal and Board meeting Nov 9, a
      follow-up with district and school leaders Nov 17, final vote expected late November.
  •    BUILDING a District-Wide Student Council. Proposed to Superintendent Dr. Reed on
      Oct 29 ("I love this idea"), discussed with him in person Nov 4, then opened to every
      middle and high school, grades 6–12. Patrick Marsh signed on Nov 5 with 3–5 Peer
      Ambassadors, Prairie Phoenix's principal wrote that he is supportive, and Sun Prairie East
      offered its weekly newsletter and SP30, then said on Nov 10 it can't share the invitation
      until the council is school-sponsored. Still open: how a district-level group registers and
      who sponsors it.
  •    ANSWERED how Board student reps are chosen. Two-year terms; recruiting starts
      in December, applications open mid-January for sophomores.
  •    ANSWERED IN WRITING license plate readers. SPPD's written FAQ (Oct 16) and the
      Chief's Oct 27 answers: a 14-day Axon retention trial, no livestreaming, monthly audits,
      and a public data-sharing MOU after signing. These are department statements, not
      contract terms yet.
  •    ASKED IN WRITING plate reader records. Public records request for the Axon
      estimate, Policy F-18, data-sharing agreements and audit records, filed Nov 9. The Chief
      also said he's open to a quarterly transparency report once Axon is approved.
  •   WAITING the Mayor. Meeting requested Oct 2.

 How to use this record
 Part 1 has every contact and every meeting in order. Parts 2–4 cover school district work,
 Part 5 the license plate reader project, Part 6 emails with the city, police, state and district,
 and Parts 7–8 the surveys and the public Student Voice pages. Emails appear as cards with
 the subject, sender, recipients and date. Anything marked as a department or district
 statement is what officials said, not a signed policy or contract.




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                                ↑ Contents      Page 8
WHAT HAPPENED, WHY I STARTED & WHY IT MATTERS




 Glossary
  • Act 95 — Wisconsin law that guarantees UW admission to top-ranked students, which is
    why class rank and how GPA is calculated matter.
  • ALPR — Automated license plate reader: a camera that photographs passing cars and
    reads the plate.
  • Axon — The company Sun Prairie plans to switch to for plate readers, starting May 2027.
  • Capstone — An advanced, project-based course; part of the weighted grades plan.
  • F-18 — SPPD's written policy on license plate readers.
  • Flock — Flock Safety, the company behind Sun Prairie's current plate reader cameras.
  • Fusus — Software that can pull camera feeds into a live view. The Chief said there will be
    no livestreaming.
  • Hotlist — A list of plates police are looking for, such as stolen cars. A match is an alert, not
    proof of a crime.
  • MOU — Memorandum of understanding: a written agreement between agencies, here
    about sharing plate data.
  • PSC — The City of Sun Prairie's Public Safety Committee.
  • SP-ALPR — Sun Prairie ALPR, Zackary's independent project on the city's plate readers
    (sunprairiealpr.netlify.app).
  • SPASD — Sun Prairie Area School District.
  • SPPD — Sun Prairie Police Department.
  • SPWHS / SPW — Sun Prairie West High School. SPE is Sun Prairie East.
  • T&L Committee — The Board of Education's Teaching & Learning Committee.
  • Weighted grades — A GPA scale where harder classes (AP, capstones, some dual credit)
    can count up to 5.0 instead of 4.0.
  • 287(g) — A federal program that lets local agencies help with immigration enforcement.
    SPPD says plate data won't be shared with agencies in it.
  • Committee of the Whole — A Board of Education meeting where all members discuss an
    item in depth before a formal vote.
  • MTSS — Multi-tiered system of support: a school's layered approach to academic and
    emotional help, from all students to those needing the most.
  • Outpost — Axon's solar-powered plate reader camera, the model in the draft Sun Prairie
    estimate.
  • Peer Ambassadors — A Patrick Marsh student group whose members may join the
    student council pilot.
  • PLC — Professional Learning Center, 220 Kroncke Dr., where the Board of Education
    meets.
  • SP30 — Sun Prairie East's daily advisory period, where announcements and slides are
    shared.
  • Public records request — A request under Wisconsin law for copies of government
    records.

1. CONTACTS & MEETINGS

 Community Report (October 8, 2026)

 Automated License Plate Readers in Sun Prairie
 Program overview • Policy & oversight • Contract & cost • Wisconsin comparison • How to
 participate
 Prepared by — Zack Mark — independent; not affiliated with the City of Sun Prairie or the Sun
 Prairie Police Department
 Last updated — October 8, 2026




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                           ↑ Contents       Page 23
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 Information current through — Flock usage log through Sept. 21, 2026 (25 new items since
 May 22, 2026); the signed 2023 Flock agreement, order form, and insurance exhibit; Oct. 7,
 2026 Public Safety Committee meeting materials (agenda, Axon budgetary estimate, Chief's
 legal memo); news reports through Oct. 8, 2026
 Questions or corrections — zackarymark160@gmail.com • 608-940-7356 • Facebook: Zack
 Mark
 Important notice: This is an independent, unofficial community summary compiled from
 public records, policy documents, and published news reports, with research and drafting
 assistance from an AI tool (Claude). It is not a City of Sun Prairie or Police Department
 publication, and it is not legal, financial, or investment advice. See Section 17 for full
 disclaimers and sources, including more on the AI assistance used.
 Language access: A summary, FAQ, and how-to-participate section are also available in
 Español and Hmoob near the end of this document.

 Executive Summary
 Sun Prairie Police have used Flock Safety license plate reader (ALPR) cameras since
 December 2023 — nine cameras today, six owned by the department and three donated by
 Woodmans. The department credits the system with stolen-vehicle recoveries and help in
 missing-person, shooting, and homicide cases, and says it follows written Policy F-18 (30-day
 retention, documented searches, alert verification, audits, and no federal sharing without a
 court order or the Chief's approval).
 The city will not renew Flock (six cameras end Dec. 31, 2026; three end Mar. 31, 2027) and
 plans to move to Axon Outpost cameras in 2027. Chief Kevin Warych briefed the Public
 Safety Committee on October 7, 2026 on the Axon Outpost transition; this was a staff report,
 not a vote, and no contract has been approved yet. A non-binding Axon budgetary estimate
 obtained since that meeting puts the proposed 9-camera system at roughly $235,389 over a
 10-year term (May 2027–April 2037, about $23,539/year) — details in Section 7. The change
 follows a wave of Wisconsin communities dropping or restricting Flock.
 Open questions residents are raising: cost versus benefit, how success is measured, audit
 transparency, data-sharing scope, contract control, and whether the program could expand
 without public approval. Notably, Axon markets Outpost as combining plate reading with live
 video streaming and vehicle-attribute recognition, unified through its Fusus platform — the
 budgetary estimate obtained so far only itemizes ALPR/"Vehicle Intelligence" hardware and
 software, with no Fusus or livestreaming line item, but that is not confirmation those
 capabilities are excluded from the final contract. Section 14 explains how to take part.

 1. Overview & Camera Network
 Sun Prairie Police Department (SPPD) has used squad-mounted plate readers since roughly
 2009 and a fixed Flock Safety camera network since December 4, 2023. Cameras photograph
 passing plates and vehicles and check them against agency-authorized "hotlists" (stolen
 vehicles, wanted persons, AMBER Alerts). Trained personnel can also search stored reads for
 documented investigations.
 The network has 9 cameras: 6 owned by SPPD (term ends December 31, 2026) and 3
 donated by Woodmans (term ends March 31, 2027).

 3. How the System Has Been Used
 From SPPD's internal tracking of notable outcomes (December 2023 – September 2026):

 Stolen vehicle recoveries
 Dozens of hits leading to recovered vehicles, several tied to weapons or drug seizures, and
 one possible link to a Madison homicide investigation (May 2024).




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                           ↑ Contents    Page 25
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 Missing persons & homicide cases
 In a January 2025 case, a missing person's vehicle was captured before and after her
 disappearance; her body was later found near Flock cameras and a suspect was charged with
 first-degree intentional homicide. A September 2026 Sun Prairie shooting and a March 2026
 Madison attempted homicide were also aided by camera hits.

 Retail theft, fraud & robbery
 Vehicles identified in shoplifting, credit-card fraud, an armed robbery (Sept. 2025), and a
 $79,000 ATM "jackpotting" case (Dec. 2025).

 Hit-and-runs & fleeing vehicles
 Vehicles identified when witnesses gave partial or incorrect plates, including a fatal
 pedestrian hit-and-run in Green County (Mar. 2026).

 Mutual aid
 Shared reads assisted Madison, Deforest, Middleton, Verona, Cottage Grove, Dane County,
 and Green County.

 Warrants, domestic cases & threats
 Used to locate wanted subjects, domestic-violence suspects, and people who threatened
 others.
 Not every hit ends in an arrest: records also show outdated "stolen" flags, vehicles already
 recovered, welfare checks, and civil matters. These outcomes come from the department's
 own summaries and were not independently verified.

 Updated log: through September 21, 2026
 SPPD's "Flock Utilization & Hits" log, updated through Sept. 21, 2026, adds 25 new notable
 items since the last update on May 22, 2026. Recent additions include stolen-vehicle hits
 (June–Sept. 2026), a Sept. 7 attempted-homicide case in Madison where the vehicle hit a Sun
 Prairie camera, a Sept. 9 Sun Prairie shooting with one suspect taken into custody, and
 several retail-theft and warrant cases. A handwritten note on the copy reviewed reads "only
 outcomes": the log lists notable outcomes, not every hit.
 What the log does and doesn't show: because it lists only notable outcomes, there is no total
 of how many alerts were wrong or went nowhere, so a success rate can't be calculated from
 it. The log itself, though, includes cases where the system's output didn't pan out: a "stolen"
 flag the originating agency never removed (Dec. 2023); a vehicle ruled out as the suspect
 vehicle in a fatal hit-and-run (Mar. 2026); an owner who entered the wrong plate as stolen
 (May 2026); and several stolen-vehicle alerts that turned out to be civil disputes or vehicles
 the owner had already recovered. Asking for error and false-alert counts is a reasonable
 request.

5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 4. National Standards: The 13 Principles
 At a September 23, 2026 Senate Judiciary Subcommittee hearing, national law enforcement
 groups (including the International Association of Chiefs of Police, the National Sheriffs'
 Organization, the National District Attorneys Association, and the National Fusion Center
 Association) released 13 recommended ALPR principles. SPPD states it follows all 13.
  • Written policies on use, access, retention, sharing, and audits
  • Permitted uses limited to trained, authorized personnel for legitimate purposes
  • Authorized access, removed when no longer needed
  • Auditing & accountability, with discipline up to termination for misuse
  • Required training for anyone with access
  • Documented queries tied to a case number or public-safety purpose
  • Verification of plate matches before enforcement action
  • Confidentiality of ALPR data from public disclosure
  • Meaningful retention balancing investigations and privacy
  • Responsible data sharing, with no selling of data
  • Equal restrictions on access to non-governmental ALPR data
  • Data security requirements for anyone storing ALPR data
 The summary sheet provided names 12 of the principles; the 13th was not on the pages
 reviewed.

 5. Local Policy & Oversight (Policy F-18)
 SPPD Policy F-18, Automatic License Plate Readers, was last updated March 3, 2026 (first
 adopted 2010). Key provisions:
  • Background checks: CIB and FBI fingerprint checks before anyone gets access.
  • Alert verification: officers must visually confirm the plate and verify vehicle status
    (NCIC/TIME) before enforcement action, and note this in reports.
  • Documented searches: every search needs a case number, a standardized incident
    category, a stated reason, and a lawful objective.
  • Retention: 30 days unless needed for an investigation.
  • Auditing: a System Administrator compiles periodic audit reports (reads, alerts,
    deployments, violations); frequency is set in a separate procedure approved by the Chief.
    Meeting notes indicate audits roughly monthly to weekly.
  • Case type and number required: meeting notes say that since about November 2025 every
    search must carry a case type, case number, and reason. The same notes mention an
    internal investigation involving misuse; the details were not captured, so that is worth
    asking about directly.
  • How audits work: notes describe audits done monthly (often weekly) with help from the
    department's records-management system, which flags triggers such as the same plate
    being searched repeatedly; history is archived. A handwritten note from the July 10
    briefing appears to record that no misuse had been found in the prior year. Confirm this
    with the department before relying on it.




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                               ↑ Contents    Page 27
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




  • Camera locations: notes say SPPD picks the locations, with an "equity lens," consistency
    across the city, and cameras strictly on roadways.
  • Privacy safeguards: logged, restricted access; no surveillance without reasonable
    suspicion of a crime; no monitoring by race, religion, gender, or political affiliation; annual
    public transparency reports.
  • What is captured: plates and vehicles only — no facial recognition, and no detection of
    people, gender, or race.
  • Prohibited uses: immigration enforcement, traffic enforcement, harassment, use based
    solely on a protected class, and personal use.

 6. Data Sharing & Federal Access
  • Data may be shared through the department-approved network with designated law
    enforcement agencies — about 200+ agencies within roughly a 50-mile radius, per the
    portal and meeting notes.
  • No sharing with federal agencies unless required by court order or expressly approved by
    the Chief.
  • Release to non-law-enforcement parties is prohibited without a court order, subpoena, or
    the Chief's approval; public-records requests go through the department's open-records
    process.
  • SPPD states its data is owned by the department and never sold.
  • 287(g) question: notes from the Oct. 7 meeting jot down sharing partners that participate
    in the restricted 287(g) program with ICE, with a figure of "13–14" written next to it. The
    notes don't make clear exactly what that number counts, so the precise count and which
    agencies are involved should be confirmed with the department.
  • Under Axon: notes say sharing would run through a formal MOU (agreement with
    protections) with each agency, and that while Flock is by far the most common system
    among nearby agencies, Axon's ALPR network is not large. That could mean fewer
    neighbors to share with.

 7. Cost & Vendor Contract
 Term — Detail
 Agreement — Flock Group, Inc. Master Services Agreement dated Sept. 8, 2023; signature

 Term / renewal — 24-month initial term that starts at first installation and validation of the
 hardware; auto-renews for another 24 months (the "greater of one year or the order-form
 length") unless either side gives 30 days' notice before the term ends; the city has said it is
 not renewing
 Order form (2023) — 5 Falcon units; $15,000/yr platform fee + $750 one-time setup =
 $15,750 Year 1; $30,750 over 2 years; Net 30
 Current cost (meeting notes) — About $3,000 per camera per year — roughly $18,000/yr for

Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                               ↑ Contents     Page 28
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 Contract terms residents have asked about
  • Hardware ownership: Flock owns the physical cameras; the city cannot move or alter them
    without Flock, and relocation carries a reinstall fee.
  • Camera placement: planned jointly, but Flock has final say and can veto a location.
  • Data ownership vs. Flock's access: the city owns its data and Flock may not sell it, but the
    contract lets Flock itself disclose footage to law enforcement or government when legally
    required or in good faith for legal process, security, fraud, or emergencies — separate from
    SPPD's own access rules.
  • Anonymized data: Flock may use de-identified data to improve its products and train
    machine-learning models.
  • Liability: capped at fees paid in the prior 12 months; Flock disclaims responsibility for
    "repeat criminal activity" or failure to capture footage.
  • Exit: the city may terminate for non-appropriation of funds on 30 days' notice; either side
    may end the agreement over an uncured material breach.

 More fine print from the 2023 Flock agreement
 Additional pages of the signed agreement and order form have since been reviewed. Several
 terms are standard vendor boilerplate, but residents asking "what exactly did the city agree
 to?" may want to know about them:
  • Permitted purpose: the service is licensed "solely for the awareness, prevention, and
    prosecution of crime, bona fide investigations and evidence gathering for law enforcement
    purposes." Flock may suspend service if it believes the system is used for anything else.
  • Who can log in: only the city's officers, employees, or agents, using city-issued email
    addresses; the city is responsible for everything its users do. Footage lives with a
    third-party cloud host, and the contract says Flock may use third parties for hosting and
    cellular service.
  • Retention is Flock's default, not a legal floor: Flock deletes footage on a rolling 30-day
    basis, and the city is responsible for downloading and archiving anything it needs to keep
    longer.
  • Audio and video language: the agreement's opening recitals describe Flock's platform as
    capable of capturing "audio, video, image, and recording data," and the definition of Flock
    Services includes "audio detection." The order form itself lists only the Falcon plate-reader
    camera, and meeting notes indicate live audio is a Flock capability that SPPD does not use.
  • Network features listed on the order form: state and nationwide license-plate lookup
    across cameras that have opted in, search by "vehicle fingerprint" (make, color, stickers,
    roof racks), search by full, partial, or temporary plates, unlimited custom hotlists, and
    real-time NCIC alerts. The packet does not say which of these SPPD has switched on.
  • Fees: Flock may change fees for a renewal term with 60 days' notice; unpaid balances can
    accrue 1% per month, and Flock can suspend service on an undisputed invoice more than
    30 days overdue (after 30 days' written notice).
  • Support and outages: Flock promises "commercially reasonable" efforts to answer support
    requests within 72 hours and to repair or replace defective hardware within 7 business
    days of inspection. Services are provided "as is"; for outages caused by third-party
    services or maintenance, the city's remedy is extra days added to the term, not a refund.


Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                               ↑ Contents       Page 29
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




  • Hardware and installs: the city may not move or tamper with cameras, or the warranty is
    void and the agreement can be terminated for breach. If cameras are lost or damaged,
    replacement is at a posted reinstall fee. Flock can remove the hardware if the city misses
    payments.
  • Indemnity and insurance: Flock indemnifies the city for patent/copyright claims and for
    property damage or injury directly caused by its installation, capped at the prior 12
    months of fees. Flock must carry general liability ($1M per occurrence / $2M aggregate),
    umbrella ($10M), professional liability/E&O ($5M), and commercial auto ($1M) coverage,
    plus cyber liability ($5M).
  • "Morality" clause: if the customer or its agents become the subject of an indictment,
    contempt, scandal, or similar event that would "negatively impact or tarnish Flock's
    reputation," Flock may terminate on written notice.
  • Publicity and feedback: Flock may use the city's name and trademarks in its marketing,
    and any suggestions or feedback from the city or its users are assigned to Flock, including
    intellectual-property rights.
  • Assignment: either side may assign the agreement without the other's consent to an
    affiliate or to a buyer or successor in a merger or sale of the business.
  • Governing law: the law and courts of the state where the customer is located (Wisconsin).
    The agreement also says a purchase is not contingent on any promised future features.
 Source: Flock Group, Inc. Master Services Agreement, Order Form (Exhibit A), Billing
 Schedule, and Exhibit B (Insurance), DocuSign envelope ID AF1416CF…; signature page
 dated Oct. 6, 2023.
 A question about dates. The 2023 order form's 24-month term starts at installation (Dec. 4,
 2023), which would point to roughly December 2025 for the end of the first term and a
 renewal term running to about December 2027. The officially reported end dates are Dec. 31,
 2026 (six cameras) and Mar. 31, 2027 (three Woodmans-donated cameras), and the order
 form covers only five cameras, not nine. That suggests later order forms or amendments
 exist that were not part of the pages reviewed. They are worth asking the city for.

 8. How Wisconsin Communities Compare
 Sun Prairie's decision comes amid a statewide reassessment of Flock. In August 2026 Flock
 announced it was cutting its recommended default retention for identifiable data from 30
 days to 7. As reported through late September 2026:
 Community — Action — Notes
 Sun Prairie — Not renewing Flock; Axon Outpost contract goes to committee Oct. 7 — Keeps
 ALPR with a new vendor; 30-day retention now, 15–21 days discussed for Axon
 Dane County Sheriff — Board pulled Flock funding; contract expired May 31; cameras
 covered — Board later restored $80,000 to find a new ALPR provider
 Stoughton — Council voted unanimously in July to end Flock —
 Fitchburg — Ended Flock — Weighing Axon and Motorola; concern over inability to track other
 agencies' use of its data
 Oshkosh — Ended Flock — Police chief learned the system could map vehicle movements,
 contrary to what a company representative had said (Wisconsin Examiner)
 Verona, Monona, Appleton, Sturgeon Bay, UW–Madison — Ended or declined to renew —
 Kenosha — Switching to Axon — Similar path to Sun Prairie
 Racine / Racine County — Council voted to terminate (mayor broke a 7–7 tie); sheriff
 removed cameras — Some agencies reported Flock kept data longer than contracts allowed;
 Flock says records are deleted per customer agreements
 Waukesha, Oconomowoc — Waukesha to stop funding after 2026; Oconomowoc ended — A
 lawsuit challenges Flock use in Waukesha County

5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 Milwaukee Police — Still uses Flock, with new limits — Supervisor-only access, specified case
 categories, no sharing with outside agencies; the county executive does not plan to renew
 county funding
 Common themes: retention length, access by out-of-area agencies, audit transparency, trust
 in the vendor, and how much control the city keeps. Sun Prairie is among those leaving Flock
 but, like Kenosha, continuing ALPR under a different vendor — so the terms of any new
 contract matter as much as the vendor's name.

 9. Open Questions & Concerns
 Questions prepared for a July 10, 2026 briefing with Chief Warych, and concerns residents
 have raised, include:
 Vendor selection & success measures: Were alternatives considered before Flock, and how is
 success measured beyond individual case stories?
 Legal review & scope: What Fourth Amendment review was done, and could the system
 expand without public approval? Courts have generally not found ALPR collection itself illegal,
 but "mission creep" is a common worry. Chief Warych's Oct. 7, 2026 memo to the Public
 Safety Committee cited a specific body of case law on this point: Wisconsin's City of Horicon
 v. Albert (1999) and State v. Folkman (2013); Seventh Circuit decisions United States v.
 Tuggle (2021), United States v. House (2024), and United States v. Hammond (2021), plus a
 pending Seventh Circuit ALPR appeal, Scholl v. Illinois State Police (argued Sept. 29, 2026);
 U.S. Supreme Court precedents United States v. Knotts (1983), United States v. Jones (2012),
 and Carpenter v. United States (2018); and two 2026 ALPR-specific rulings from other
 circuits, United States v. Porter (5th Cir.) and Schmidt v. City of Norfolk (E.D. Va.). All of these
 describe ALPR or similar vehicle-tracking technology as generally not constituting a Fourth
 Amendment "search" in the cited circumstances — none of them is a ruling on Sun Prairie's
 program specifically, and Scholl remains pending.
 Misuse & stalking risk: Policy calls for investigation of suspected misuse; some residents want
 to know whether any violations have been found. Notes mention both a mandatory
 case-type/case-number requirement since Nov. 2025 and an internal investigation involving
 misuse, and a July 10 note that appears to say no misuse was found in the prior year; the
 department can clarify how those fit together. Another open item from the notes: with Flock's
 automated system, the same plate could be wrongly flagged multiple times, so how will
 Axon's audits and automatic flagging catch errors?
 Cost vs. benefit: Support is strongest for stolen-vehicle recovery and missing-person cases; is
 the cost proportionate? A non-binding Axon estimate obtained after Oct. 7 puts the proposed
 system at about $235,389 over 10 years (roughly $23,500/year) — higher than the current
 Flock cost of about $15,000–$18,000/year, though the Axon figure also bundles two
 mid-contract hardware refreshes Flock's model didn't include. See Section 7.
 Data sharing: With 200+ agencies able to search 30 days of history, residents want clearer
 public accounting of who has looked at Sun Prairie's data and why.




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                                ↑ Contents     Page 32
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 10. The Move to Axon

 Sun Prairie's plan
  • The city will not renew Flock. Police will present an Axon Outpost contract to the Public
    Safety Committee on Oct. 7.
  • City officials say the city would own and control its ALPR data, including access and export,
    and that Axon would consolidate data storage, body-worn cameras, and TASER devices on
    one platform. Because that platform includes TASERs and body cameras, the vendor
    relationship is broader than ALPR alone.
  • Retention: Flock's is 30 days now (7 days from Jan. 1, 2027 per notes); Axon's is discussed
    at 15–21 days.
  • Axon's own materials describe agency-owned data, tamper-evident audit trails, no
    automatic federal access, plates-only capture (no facial recognition), and planned tools for
    flagging unusual searches. These are vendor statements; the contract language is what
    binds the city.

 About Axon Enterprise
 Axon Enterprise, Inc. (NASDAQ: AXON) is based in Scottsdale, Arizona. Founded in 1993 as a
 Taser maker (TASER International until renamed Axon in 2017), it now sells Tasers, body
 cameras, in-car cameras, digital evidence and records software, and, through its Fusus
 acquisition, real-time crime center and ALPR technology. It has roughly 4,000+ employees,
 several billion dollars in annual revenue, and a market value in the tens of billions.

 National context: Axon stock and the ICE Taser contract
 Separately from Sun Prairie's decision, financial disclosures show an account in President
 Donald Trump's name bought $1–5 million of Axon stock on February 10, 2026. Two weeks
 later, on February 24, 2026, U.S. Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) posted a
 federal contracting-database notice seeking information from vendors for a large Taser
 purchase.
 Details reported on the ICE contract itself:
  • Scale: ICE sought about 17,800 new Tasers — up from roughly 4,300 then in the field —
    specifically T10 models with 10-probe cartridges effective up to 45 feet, plus unlimited
    cartridges and agent training.
  • Value and term: Roughly $220 million over five years.
  • Vendor: The notice's technical specifications reportedly matched only Axon's products;
    Axon is the sole maker of conductive-energy weapons meeting ICE's stated specs. Vendors
    had until March 13, 2026 to respond, and as of this report it was not publicly confirmed
    that Axon had been formally awarded the contract.
  • Stated justification: ICE said the devices give agents "greater distance to try to de-escalate
    a hostile situation" and a better chance of a successful probe connection.
  • Criticism: Sen. Adam Schiff criticized the spending amid broader growth in ICE's
    contracting; Diane Goldstein of the Law Enforcement Action Partnership questioned
    whether Tasers would actually be used to de-escalate and noted the risk of
    electrical-shock injury.



Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                              ↑ Contents    Page 33
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 No charges have been filed and no insider trading has been proven; there is no public
 evidence the president was involved in the procurement or that Axon knew of the purchase.
 The White House says the holdings are managed by third parties and denies any conflict.
 Ethics experts cited in coverage say the concern is the appearance of a conflict, not proven
 misconduct. This involves a separate federal (ICE) procurement for Tasers — not ALPR
 cameras, and not Sun Prairie's contract — but residents may consider it relevant context
 when asking about the vendor the city is choosing for a different product line.
 Sources: Times of San Diego (Mar. 3, 2026); 24/7 Wall St. (June 29, 2026); CNBC (June 29,
 2026); NOTUS.

 11. Axon Outpost: Beyond Traditional ALPR
 Axon's own marketing describes Outpost as more than a plate-reader replacement. Axon
 says Outpost combines three capabilities in one fixed device: automated license plate
 recognition, live video streaming, and vehicle-attribute recognition (make, model, color, and
 features like roof racks, bumper stickers, or missing plates). A companion product, Axon
 Lightpost, adds the same three capabilities to existing streetlights. Axon says both feed into
 Axon Fusus, a real-time operations platform that can unify Outpost/Lightpost data with other
 connected cameras and sensors — Axon's own materials describe this as bringing "visibility
 across every fixed, mobile, and community-sourced ALPR-enabled camera."
 That means the relevant question for Sun Prairie isn't only "is Flock being replaced by
 another plate reader?" It's which of these capabilities the city is actually purchasing and
 enabling, and which rules will govern them:
  • Will livestreaming and vehicle-attribute recognition be turned on, or only plate reading?
  • If vehicle attributes are collected, are they searchable without a known plate, and how
    long are they retained?
  • Is Axon Fusus included in the proposed contract, and if so, what other cameras or data
    sources could it connect to later?
  • Does SPPD's Policy F-18 — written for plate reads — automatically cover livestreaming and
    attribute recognition, or would it need to be updated?
  • What approval (council vote, budget process, policy amendment) would be required before
    additional cameras or capabilities are added later?
 Update: A non-binding Axon budgetary estimate obtained after the Oct. 7, 2026 committee
 meeting itemizes only ALPR camera hardware and an "Axon Vehicle Intelligence – ALPR
 License" for all 9 cameras — it does not list Fusus or a livestreaming license as separate line
 items. That leans toward "plate reading only" for the configuration priced so far, but a
 budgetary estimate isn't the final statement of work, and Outpost's base camera hardware
 may be capable of more than what's separately licensed. See Section 7 for the full cost
 breakdown.
 Sources: Axon product pages for Outpost, Lightpost, and Fusus (axon.com); Axon's 2026
 press materials on its expanded ALPR camera lineup; Axon Quote Q-932192-001 (Sept. 22,
 2026).




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                              ↑ Contents       Page 34
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 12. Flock & Axon: A Complicated History
 Because Sun Prairie is leaving one vendor for the other, it's worth knowing the two
 companies have a tangled relationship:
  • 2020: Axon made a strategic investment in Flock and the companies partnered, offering
    Flock's fixed ALPR alongside Axon's Fleet in-car systems.
  • Ongoing (pre-2025): Flock's Vehicle Fingerprint technology was integrated into Axon Fleet
    3, letting Flock-derived evidence flow automatically into Axon Evidence.
  • August 28, 2024: the companies agreed to a modestly expanded integration involving
    Fusus and Flock devices.
  • Early-mid 2025: Flock says Axon unilaterally decided to end the API partnership, telling
    Flock that continued openness was "no longer in Axon's best interests."
  • Since July 24, 2025: per Flock, Axon no longer supports new mutual customers, though
    existing customers may still get some support — Flock says the practical effect on
    customers is minimal since it opened its own APIs at no cost.
 Flock's partner directory still listed an "Axon (Evidence.com)" integration as of this report,
 which is worth reconciling directly with both companies rather than assuming either
 description is current. Because Sun Prairie is moving to Axon's own Outpost hardware (not
 continuing to run Flock alongside Axon), this history mostly matters for one practical
 question: will Sun Prairie's historical Flock data be transferred, exported, or simply left behind
 when the Flock cameras come down.
 Sources: Flock Safety, "Axon Plans to Sever APIs With Flock" (June 2025); Axon's 2020
 partnership announcement.

 13. Key Questions for the Committee & Vendors
 Status as of Oct. 8, 2026: The Oct. 7 Public Safety Committee meeting was an informational
 update from Chief Warych, not an action item, and the questions below remain largely open.
 A non-binding Axon cost estimate (Section 7) partially answers the cost, camera-count, and
 "is Fusus included" questions, but the city has not published a signed contract, final
 camera-placement plan, or final retention figure. Beyond the open questions in Section 9, the
 added complexity of Outpost/Fusus and the Flock-Axon history raise a longer list worth
 putting to the Public Safety Committee, Axon, and Flock directly. Condensed:

 For the Public Safety Committee / SPPD
  • How many Outpost cameras, and where? What is the total contract cost and term,
    including renewal and termination provisions?
  • Is Fusus included? Are livestreaming and vehicle-attribute recognition enabled?
  • What is the retention period for each data type (plate reads, attributes, video, audit logs),
    including after contract termination?
  • Will historical Flock data be transferred, exported to the city, or left with Flock?
  • Who can access the data — other agencies, federal agencies, Axon employees or
    subcontractors — and is that access logged?
  • Can Axon use city data (including de-identified data) for its own product development or AI
    training?



Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                                ↑ Contents   Page 35
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




  • Will the city publish annual audit statistics and the full contract, including data-processing
    and security terms?
  • Does Policy F-18 need to be amended to cover the new capabilities, and what approval
    would future expansion require?
 Full, unedited question lists the author prepared for Chief Kevin Warych, Flock Safety, and
 Axon are available on request via the contact information in Section 13.

 15. Glossary
 Term — Meaning
 ALPR — Automated License Plate Reader — a camera system that photographs plates and
 checks them against watch lists.
 Hotlist — A list of plates flagged for a reason (stolen vehicle, wanted person, AMBER Alert)
 that triggers an alert when read.
 Retention period — How long the system keeps a plate read before automatically deleting it,
 absent a hold for an investigation.
 Flock Safety — The company that has supplied Sun Prairie's current ALPR cameras since
 2023.
 Axon Enterprise — The company (maker of Tasers and body cameras) that Sun Prairie plans
 to switch to for ALPR in 2027.
 Axon Outpost — Axon's fixed camera product, combining plate reading, live video, and
 vehicle-attribute recognition.
 Axon Lightpost — A version of Outpost's capabilities built to install on existing streetlights.
 Axon Fusus — Axon's platform for combining feeds from Outpost/Lightpost with other
 connected cameras and sensors into one view.
 Vehicle-attribute recognition — Identifying a vehicle by features other than its plate — color,
 make, model, roof racks, bumper stickers, etc.
 Vehicle Fingerprint — Flock's version of vehicle-attribute recognition.
 NCIC — National Crime Information Center — the FBI's national database of wanted persons
 and stolen vehicles.
 CJIS — Criminal Justice Information Services — the FBI's security standards for handling
 criminal-justice data.
 287(g) — A federal program allowing certain local agencies to perform some
 immigration-enforcement functions; referenced here because it's one channel by which ALPR
 data could reach federal immigration authorities.




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                               ↑ Contents    Page 37
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 Open records law — Wisconsin's public-records law, which lets residents request government
 records — though ALPR data itself is generally treated as confidential and exempt.
 Public Safety Committee — The Common Council committee that reviews police- and
 fire-related proposals before they go to the full council.
 Common Council — Sun Prairie's elected city council, which gives final approval to most
 contracts of this size.
 Policy F-18 — SPPD's internal written policy governing ALPR use, access, and retention.

 16. Plain-English FAQ

 Does this use facial recognition?
 No. SPPD and Flock both state the system reads plates and vehicles only, not faces, gender,
 or race.

 Can ICE or other federal agencies see Sun Prairie's data?
 Under current policy, no — not without a court order or the Chief's specific approval. Whether
 that same limit will be written into the Axon contract is one of the open questions in this
 report.

 Does the city own the cameras?
 Currently, no — Flock owns the hardware even though SPPD paid for most of it and
 Woodman's donated three units. Whether Sun Prairie or Axon will own the new cameras
 hasn't been confirmed.

 How long is my car's plate data kept?
 30 days under the current policy and contract, unless it's tied to an active investigation. Flock
 has said it's moving toward 7 days; Axon's proposed retention (15–21 days, per earlier notes)
 hasn't been finalized in a contract yet.

 Is this legal?
 Courts have generally not found ALPR data collection itself illegal. That doesn't resolve every
 question residents have raised about retention length, scope, or oversight — those are policy
 questions, not settled legal ones.

 Could the city add more cameras or capabilities later without another public
 vote?
 That depends on the contract language, which is one of the biggest open questions in this
 report (see Section 13).

 What does this cost?
 The 2023 Flock contract ran about $15,000–$18,000 a year depending on which figures you
 use. A non-binding Axon budgetary estimate obtained after the Oct. 7 meeting puts the
 proposed 9-camera Outpost system at about $235,389 total over a 10-year term
 (2027–2037), or roughly $23,500/year — but it's explicitly marked a draft, non-binding
 estimate, not a signed contract, so the final number could change.



Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                              ↑ Contents    Page 38
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 Can I find out if my car's plate has been read?
 Not directly — ALPR data is treated as confidential, and routine public-records requests for it
 are typically denied. You can ask the department directly about its process.

 Who do I talk to if I have more questions?
 See Section 14, "How to Weigh In," for city contacts, or reach the author of this report
 directly.

 19. Resumen en Español
 Nota: esta traducción fue generada con ayuda de inteligencia artificial y cubre únicamente el
 resumen, las preguntas frecuentes y cómo participar — no el informe completo. Si nota algún
 error, por favor contáctenos.
 Aviso importante: Este es un resumen comunitario independiente y no oficial preparado por
 Zack Mark. No está afiliado con la Ciudad de Sun Prairie, el Departamento de Policía de Sun
 Prairie, Flock Safety ni Axon Enterprise. Esto no es asesoría legal ni financiera.

 Resumen
 El Departamento de Policía de Sun Prairie (SPPD) ha usado cámaras lectoras automáticas de
 placas (ALPR) de la empresa Flock Safety desde diciembre de 2023. Actualmente hay 9
 cámaras: 6 propiedad del departamento y 3 donadas por Woodman's. La ciudad no renovará
 el contrato con Flock y planea cambiar a cámaras Axon Outpost en 2027. El 7 de octubre de
 2026, el Jefe Kevin Warych presentó un informe (no una votación) sobre la transición a Axon
 ante el Comité de Seguridad Pública; aún no se ha aprobado ningún contrato. Un presupuesto
 preliminar y no vinculante de Axon, obtenido después de la reunión, estima el costo total en
 aproximadamente $235,389 dólares durante 10 años (2027–2037) para las 9 cámaras.

 Preguntas frecuentes
 ¿Usa reconocimiento facial? No. El sistema solo lee placas y vehículos, no rostros.
 ¿Puede el gobierno federal (como ICE) ver los datos? Bajo la política actual, no, sin una orden
 judicial o aprobación específica del Jefe de Policía.
 ¿Cuánto tiempo se guardan los datos? 30 días bajo la política y el contrato actuales, salvo
 que estén vinculados a una investigación activa.
 ¿Es esto legal? Los tribunales generalmente no han determinado que la recolección de datos
 ALPR sea ilegal, pero persisten preguntas sobre políticas y supervisión.

 Cómo participar
  • Próximas reuniones: la reunión del 7 de octubre ya ocurrió (solo informe, sin votación);
    revise las agendas futuras del Comité de Seguridad Pública y del Concejo Municipal en
    cityofsunprairie.com o sunprairiemediacenter.com para la próxima oportunidad de
    comentar.
  • Oficina del Secretario Municipal (City Clerk): (608) 837-2511.
  • Preguntas o correcciones sobre este informe: Zack Mark — zackarymark160@gmail.com •
    608-940-7356 • Facebook: Zack Mark.

 el contrato, y una comparación con otras comunidades de Wisconsin.

 Open

 Does Policy F-18 cover the new features?
 It was written for plate reads. It is unclear whether it covers livestreaming or vehicle-attribute
 search.
 Open

 What approval would adding cameras or features need?
 Not stated in any public document so far.
 Open

 Do the Flock contract dates add up?
 The 2023 order form covers 5 cameras on a 24-month term from installation. The reported
 end dates are Dec. 31, 2026 and Mar. 31, 2027 for 9 cameras. Later order forms or
 amendments probably exist and haven't been seen.
 Open

 Briefing Memo (July 10)
 SUN PRAIRIE FLOCK ALPR BRIEFING MEMO Meeting with Chief Kevin Warych | July 10, 2026 |
 Prepared by Zack Mark

 Purpose of Meeting
 To better understand how the Sun Prairie Police Department uses Flock Safety Automated
 License Plate Reader (ALPR) cameras, including public safety benefits, privacy implications,
 oversight, data sharing, accountability, and community trust.

 What Sun Prairie Has Communicated So Far
  • Sun Prairie uses Flock Safety ALPR cameras. Sun Prairie's public Flock transparency page
    states that the system is used to help solve and reduce crime and that it captures license
    plates and vehicle images.
  • The transparency page states that the system is not used for traffic enforcement, personal
    data collection, or surveillance based on race or other protected characteristics.
  • The page also states that data is owned by Sun Prairie PD and is never sold to third parties.
  • Sun Prairie's transparency page states that audit reports and event logs are accessible to
    system administrators to help ensure appropriate use.
  • In prior communication and public discussion, it has been indicated that Sun Prairie
    currently has nine cameras in use and that local use of the system is limited. That number
    should still be confirmed directly in the meeting.
 [1] Flock Safety, "Sun Prairie WI PD Transparency Portal," last updated July 8, 2026,
 https://transparency.flocksafety.com/sun-prairie-wi-pd [2] Prior direct email communication /
 meeting setup context with Chief Kevin Warych.

 Main Public Safety Benefits Raised by Community Members
 Residents have shared several examples of why they support Flock cameras and why they
 view them as a valuable tool:
  • recovery of stolen vehicles
  • locating missing persons / Silver Alert subjects
  • assisting violent crime investigations
  • general deterrence / neighborhood safety
  • providing investigative leads when time matters
 These examples are resident accounts shared in public comments, not independently verified
 Sun Prairie PD case summaries. They are useful as examples of perceived benefits and
 community support, but should not be presented as official outcome data unless confirmed
 by the department.
 [3] Public Facebook comments submitted by Sun Prairie residents in response to Zack Mark's
 July 10 meeting post. [4] Flock Safety, "Privacy & Ethics," describing common ALPR
 public-safety use cases, https://www.flocksafety.com/privacy-ethics-copy




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                             ↑ Contents    Page 51
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 Major Community Concerns Raised
 The strongest concerns from residents were not just about the existence of cameras, but
 about how the data is governed, shared, and potentially abused.
 Privacy / surveillance concerns
  • concern that the system can reconstruct patterns of life, including where people go, when
    they leave, and when they return
  • concern that cameras may capture activity near parks, sidewalks, walking routes, and
    other public spaces
 Misuse / stalking / domestic violence concerns
  • concern that officers or authorized users could abuse the system to track ex-partners,
    romantic interests, domestic violence survivors, or other individuals for personal reasons
 Data sharing and outside access
  • questions about sharing with other Dane County agencies, statewide partners, or federal
    agencies
  • concern about broader network-sharing arrangements and whether local settings could
    expand over time
 Vendor control / future policy changes
  • concern about "mission creep," leadership changes, software-setting changes, or contract
    changes quietly broadening use
 Cost / necessity / alternatives
  • questions about annual cost, long-term contract cost, alternatives, and whether benefits
    justify privacy and financial tradeoffs
 [5] Flock Safety, "Data Privacy & Retention Policies,"
 https://www.flocksafety.com/trust/data-privacy [6] Associated Press, "License plate camera
 company halts cooperation with federal agencies among investigation concerns," Sept. 2025,
 https://apnews.com/article/cc5f29df94a29ee2c6c2feb2151c8f5e

 Reported National / Non-Local Misuse Examples Raised by Residents
 One resident posted examples involving misuse of ALPR/Flock-related systems by law
 enforcement in other jurisdictions. These examples were raised as reasons for concern about
 abuse and oversight. They were not presented as Sun Prairie incidents, but as examples of
 what can happen if safeguards fail.
 The clearest verified Wisconsin example is Milwaukee: former officer Josue Ayala was publicly
 reported to have used Milwaukee's Flock system to track a former romantic partner and
 another individual for personal reasons. Other examples raised by residents from Kansas,
 Texas, Florida, and Georgia should be independently verified before being cited in any
 public-facing summary.
 [7] FOX6 Milwaukee, report on Josue Ayala Flock misuse case,
 https://www.fox6now.com/news/milwaukee-police-officer-charged-flock-camera-misuse-case
 [8] Wisconsin Examiner, report on Milwaukee officer accused of misusing Flock cameras, http
 s://wisconsinexaminer.com/2026/02/26/milwaukee-officer-accused-of-misusing-flock-surveilla


Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                            ↑ Contents    Page 52
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 nce-cameras/ [9] WISN 12 News, report on guilty plea / restraining orders in Ayala case, https
 ://www.wisn.com/article/court-grants-restraining-orders-against-ex-cop-who-stalked-ex-with-fl
 ock-cameras/71709296

 Themes Emerging From Supportive Comments
 Even among residents who support Flock, support is often conditional rather than unlimited.
 Common themes include support when used for serious public safety purposes, comfort with
 plate capture but not with misuse, greater trust in local leadership than in vendors or broad
 systems, and strong concern about access controls, especially whether use is logged and
 whether those logs are actually reviewed.

 Themes Emerging From Opposed Comments
 Residents opposed to Flock generally framed their objections as concerns about government
 overreach, private company control of public data, constitutional / Fourth Amendment issues,
 distrust of future policy changes, and a belief that communities should not expand
 surveillance infrastructure absent a clear demonstrated need.

 Practical Issues to Clarify During the Meeting
  • who has access to Sun Prairie's Flock system
  • whether dispatchers or non-sworn staff have access
  • how often logs are audited
  • whether outside agencies can search Sun Prairie data directly
  • whether Sun Prairie is opted into any broader network-sharing settings
  • whether federal agencies can obtain or access data
  • how long data is retained
  • where data is stored
  • whether Flock itself can access or retain local data
  • how camera placement decisions are made
  • what measurable outcomes Sun Prairie has seen from the system
  • what the annual and long-term cost of the program is
 [10] Flock Safety, "Evidence Policy," https://www.flocksafety.com/legal/flock-evidence-policy

 Bottom-Line Public Tension Going Into the Meeting
 The public divide does not appear to be simply "pro-camera" versus "anti-camera." It is more
 specific. Some residents see Flock as a valuable public safety tool. Some are not opposed to
 the technology itself, but want strict limits, strong auditing, narrow access, and transparency.
 Others oppose the system more broadly because they see it as part of a larger trend toward
 government surveillance, privatized data collection, and civil-liberties erosion.
 That tension is the core of the meeting: What exactly is Sun Prairie doing with the system
 today, what safeguards exist, and what prevents that use from quietly expanding tomorrow?




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                             ↑ Contents    Page 53
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 Questions for Chief Warych (July 10)
 Sun Prairie ALPR Meeting — Zack Mark | Meeting with Chief Kevin Warych | July 10

 CORE SYSTEM + USAGE

 Purpose & Use
  • What are Sun Prairie's primary goals for using Flock ALPR cameras?
  • In what specific types of cases is the system actually used (stolen vehicles, violent crime,
    missing persons, etc.)?
  • Since adoption, how many cases has it directly helped solve or significantly advance?

 What the System Collects
  • What exact data is captured (plate, vehicle image, make/model, timestamps, location)?
  • Can the system capture anything beyond license plates (people, patterns, movement
    tracking)?
  • Is there any capability for broader tracking beyond single-event reads?

 Camera Placement & Scope
  • How are camera locations chosen?
  • Are cameras strictly roadway-focused or can they capture nearby public spaces (parks,
    sidewalks, lots)?

 Access & Internal Controls
  • Who has access to the system (officers, dispatchers, civilians)?
  • What training is required before access is granted?
  • Are searches logged and routinely audited?
  • How often are audits conducted and what do they review?

 DATA, SHARING, COST, SAFEGUARDS

 Data Sharing
  • Does Sun Prairie share or allow access to other agencies in Dane County or beyond?
  • Can outside agencies search Sun Prairie data directly, or must they request it?
  • Is Sun Prairie opted into any broader Flock network or data-sharing system?
  • Can federal agencies access or request this data? Under what conditions?

 Data Storage & Retention
  • Where is data stored (cloud, vendor system, local control)?
  • How long is data retained before deletion?
  • Who controls retention policies—Sun Prairie or Flock?
  • Is deleted data permanently removed?



Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                              ↑ Contents    Page 54
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 Oversight & Misuse Prevention
  • What safeguards exist to prevent misuse (e.g., personal tracking or unauthorized
    searches)?
  • Has any misuse ever been identified?
  • What happens if misuse occurs?
  • Are all users (including dispatch) included in auditing?

 Cost & Accountability
  • What is the annual cost of the system?
  • What does the City actually pay for (hardware, software, data access, storage)?
  • Were alternative vendors considered before selecting Flock?
  • How is success measured beyond anecdotal cases?

 Legal / Policy Framework
  • What legal review was done before adoption?
  • How are Fourth Amendment/privacy concerns addressed in policy?
  • Could this system be expanded in scope without public approval?
  • What safeguards prevent "mission creep" over time?

 COMMUNITY INPUT
 Residents have raised:
  • Privacy and surveillance concerns
  • Data sharing transparency
  • Misuse and stalking risks
  • Strong support based on stolen vehicle recovery and missing persons cases
  • Questions about long-term cost vs benefit

 Closing Question
  • What do you think the public most misunderstands about this system, and what concern
    do you think is actually valid?

 Chief Warych Meeting Brief & Notes (August 17)
 SUN PRAIRIE POLICE DEPARTMENT — Chief Kevin Warych Meeting Brief Prepared by: Zackary
 Mark. Purpose: Public safety • Transparency • Accountability • Staffing • Future priorities

 Why This Meeting
 Following our previous conversation about Flock ALPR technology, I wanted to better
 understand the broader public-safety challenges facing Sun Prairie and how SPPD is
 preparing for the future.
 My goal is to understand how the department balances effective policing, limited resources,
 transparency, and community trust.

 Key Data
 Police Department — 2026 budget
  • 86 total positions
  • 63 sworn positions
  • 23 civilian positions

 Calls for Service
 The City reports calls for service increased 13.5% from 2020–2024, averaging 70+ calls per
 day. The City has identified increasing demands involving mental-health calls and transports,
 complex investigations, crimes involving children, cyber/digital evidence, and violent crime.

 Staffing
 The City has previously identified recruitment and retention as challenges and has cited
 staffing shortages as affecting proactive policing and community engagement.

 Crime — SPPD 2024 Annual Report
  • 335 crimes against persons
  • 321 assault offenses
  • 62 aggravated assaults
  • 175 drug/narcotic offenses
  • 28 weapons-law violations
 These numbers should be considered alongside population growth, reporting patterns, and
 enforcement activity.

 Questions & Meeting Notes
 1. Staffing
 Q: How many of the department's authorized positions are currently filled? Notes: 61 of 63
 sworn (fully staffed = 63). Sworn vacancies: 2. Civilian vacancies: 1.
 Q: What positions are hardest to recruit or retain? Notes: Officers; qualifications.




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                               ↑ Contents   Page 56
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 2. Recruitment & Retention
 Q: What are the biggest reasons people choose not to join or leave SPPD? Notes: Personal,
 family, moving, culture. (None in 15 months.)
 Q: What is working best to improve recruitment and retention? Notes: Taking care of people;
 building culture.

 3. Call Volume
 Q: Calls for service reportedly increased 13.5% from 2020–2024. What is driving that
 increase, and which types of calls consume the most department resources? Notes: One-year
 average is increasing. Drivers: population increase, traffic stops, checks, assists, business
 growth, event growth, mental health needs.

 4. Proactive Policing
 Q: When staffing is limited, what proactive services are most difficult to maintain? (Examples:
 community policing, traffic enforcement, school/community engagement, investigations.)
 Notes: Traffic complaints.

 5. Accountability
 Q: How does SPPD measure police accountability and officer performance? Notes: April and
 October evaluations; 6-month at-a-glance; broken-down analytics; data not taken alone; all
 citizen complaints taken into account and tracked.
 Q: What information about complaints, use of force, or department performance is publicly
 available? Notes: All stats; Annual Report (look this up).

 6. Community Trust
 Q: How does the department measure whether residents actually trust SPPD? Notes:
 Community Satisfaction Survey on the website.
 Q: What groups or communities do you think SPPD needs to build stronger relationships with?
 Notes: All communities — through informal leaders; The Star podcast.

 7. Mental Health
 Q: How is SPPD responding to increasing mental-health-related calls? Notes: Involved in daily
 work; CARES (paramedic and social worker), about $531 every call; other resources; Journey
 (resource).
 Q: Are there situations where another response model could be more effective than
 traditional police response? Notes: Officers instead.

 8. 2026 Funding
 Q: The City proposed a $3.95 million annual levy increase in the April 2026 referendum,
 which voters rejected. What does that result mean for SPPD's staffing, services, and
 priorities? Notes: Different growth in services; no growth of even one police officer. SPPD
 budget is about $12.9 million.




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                             ↑ Contents    Page 57
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 9. Technology
 Q: What new technologies do you believe will have the biggest impact on policing over the
 next five years? Notes: ALPR; AI usage; efficiency first.
 Q: How does SPPD evaluate privacy, effectiveness, cost, and potential misuse before
 adopting technology? Notes: Trust in tech and privacy; rights; retention; viewing; storage;
 many, many rules followed; open records laws; misuse accountability.

 10. Looking Forward
 Q: What are the three biggest public-safety challenges Sun Prairie will face over the next 3–5
 years? Notes: Public safety is the biggest thing.
 1. Staffing — referendum challenge; about 700 people per year of population growth.
 2. Budget — tight; 3 years set at $12.9 million; stay under.
 3. Mindfulness of community relationships — trust is a forever priority.

 Final Question
 Q: If you could change one thing about how Sun Prairie approaches public safety over the
 next five years, what would it be? Notes: Constant listening, evolving, changing and meeting
 expectations, and building trust. "Human faith will go down for sure."

 Sources
 City of Sun Prairie — 2026 Budget; SPPD — 2024 Annual Report; City of Sun Prairie — 2026
 Referendum Information; Previous Flock ALPR Briefing Memo.

 Flock Transparency Portal (July 10 printout)
 Printout brought to the July 10 meeting.

 Sun Prairie WI PD Transparency Portal (Flock Safety) — last updated
 Fri Jul 10 2026
 Overview. The Sun Prairie Police Department uses Flock Safety's Automated License Plate
 Reader (ALPR) system to help solve and reduce crime in our community. ALPR cameras
 capture images of license plates and compare them to databases, such as "hotlists" of
 vehicles associated with crimes or missing persons. This technology allows officers to quickly
 identify vehicles of interest. The system also allows officers to search past images of vehicles
 when investigating a crime. To protect privacy, the system is not used for traffic
 enforcement, personal data collection, or surveillance based on race or other characteristics.
 Policies
  • What's Detected: License Plates, Vehicles
  • What's Not Detected: Facial recognition, People, Gender, Race
  • Acceptable Use Policy: Data is used for law enforcement purposes only. Data is owned by
    Sun Prairie WI PD and is never sold to 3rd parties. Audit reports and event logs are
    accessible to system administrators to ensure the system is used appropriately.
  • Prohibited Uses: Immigration enforcement, traffic enforcement, harassment or
    intimidation, usage based solely on a protected class (i.e. race, sex, religion), Personal use.
  • Access Policy: All system access requires a valid reason and is stored indefinitely.
  • Hotlist Policy: Hotlist hits are required to be human verified prior to action.
 Usage
  • Data Retention: 30 days
  • Total Cameras (LPR and other cameras): 9
  • Sharing Network: Organizations granted access to Sun Prairie WI PD data (list on the
    portal)
  • Hotlists Alerted On: NCIC, NCMEC Amber Alert, Wisconsin DOJ
  • Vehicles detected in the last 30 days (unique plate reads): 249,284
  • Number of hotlist hits in the last 30 days: 1,510
  • Number of searches (user search sessions) in the last 30 days: 186

 ALPR Handout & @sunprairiealpr (October 27)
 One-page handout, updated October 27, 2026, posted on sunprairiealpr.netlify.app.
 Update: later on October 27, Chief Warych answered several of the open questions below in writing:
 Axon retention will start as a 14-day, three-month trial; there will be no livestreaming; searches will be
 audited monthly; and a data-sharing MOU will be made public after signing. See his email in Part 6.

 Who reads your license plate?
 Sun Prairie police use plate reader cameras. The city plans to switch from Flock to Axon. Here
 is what is known and what is still open.
 Four facts:
  • 9 cameras today: 6 city-owned, 3 donated by Woodman's
  • 30 days: how long plate data is kept now
  • May 2027: planned start of Axon
  • $235,389: draft, non-binding Axon estimate, 10 years
 What the department says (in a FAQ, not a contract):
  • The city owns the plate data.
  • No federal access, and no sharing with agencies in the 287(g) program.
  • Every search is logged with an offense category and a reason.
 These are promises, not contract terms. The Oct. 7 meeting was a report, and no vote has
 been scheduled.
 Three questions to ask:
  • How long will Axon keep the data, and who can change that number?
  • Will those promises be written into the contract, and will the contract be published?
  • Will livestreaming or Fusus be turned on, and what approval would adding cameras need?
 The site has the full report, the documents, the timeline and a template email to the Mayor.
 Independent and unofficial; written with help from an AI assistant, so check anything
 important against the official documents. Figures are from public documents and a draft
 estimate as of Oct. 27, 2026.

 data is stored and searchable); 30 days of data kept today, with the Axon retention period

 10 years; what the department's FAQ says (the city owns the data, no federal access, every
 search is logged); and "Read it. Ask questions."

 @sunprairiealpr reels (October 29)
 Five short reels (about 17–21 seconds each), all ending on the SP-ALPR plate logo,
 sunprairiealpr.netlify.app, and "Independent. Not affiliated with the City of Sun Prairie or
 SPPD."
  • By the numbers (posted to @sunprairiealpr Oct 29): 249,284 license plates read in 30 days
    by 9 cameras; 1,510 hotlist hits and 186 searches (department portal, July 10). "A hit is an
    alert, not proof of a crime." Data kept 30 days.
  • 5 steps, explained: what happens when you drive past a plate reader. Capture, read (plate,
    color and make), match against a hotlist (an alert, not proof), keep for 30 days. No facial
    recognition, according to the department and Flock.
  • The switch: Flock is out, Axon is in, with a planned start of May 2027. $235,389 draft price
    for 9 cameras over 10 years, about $23.5K a year (draft estimate, non-binding, expires
    Nov. 30). "Now it's moving with the city budget. Budget meetings are where to speak up."
  • 4 promises: the city owns the data, no federal access, no sharing with 287(g) agencies,
    every search is logged (source: SPPD's Oct. 16 FAQ). "A FAQ is a statement of intent. A
    contract is what's binding. Put it in the contract."
  • Say something, in 3 steps: email the Mayor and the committee
    (mayor@cityofsunprairie.com); speak at public comment in Council Chambers, 300 E. Main
    St.; ask your alder where they stand (City Clerk: 608-837-2511). "For, against or
    undecided. It all counts."
