 SPPD Policy F-18
 SUN PRAIRIE POLICE DEPARTMENT POLICY AND PROCEDURE — Field Operations Policy:
 AUTOMATIC LICENSE PLATE READERS — POLICY F-18 Updated: March 3, 2026. Reviewed:
 March 3, 2026. History: Replaces F-18 (12/17/2010); updated 08/11/2014, 10/31/2016, new
 format 05/06/2025, updated 3/03/2026. 2598 West Main Street, Sun Prairie, WI 53590.

 1.1 Policy
 The Sun Prairie Police Department (SNPD) utilizes Automated License Plate Readers (ALPR) to
 enhance public safety, aid in criminal investigations, and improve law enforcement efficiency.
 ALPR capture infrared images of vehicle license plates, convert them to digital text using
 Optical Character Recognition (OCR) technology, and match them against databases of
 license plates of interest. ALPR can alert officers when a match is detected, aiding in
 identifying stolen vehicles, wanted persons, and other subjects of interest.
 This policy governs the use, management, and retention of ALPR data by SNPD personnel,
 ensuring compliance with privacy laws and safeguarding civil liberties.

 1.2 Objective
 To provide department members with guidelines on the proper use of the ALPR in their
 official duties.

 1.3 Definitions
  • ALPR (Automated License Plate Reader): A system consisting of cameras and technology
    that captures images of vehicle license plates, converts them to digital text using OCR,
    and compares them to databases of vehicles of interest.
  • OCR (Optical Character Recognition): Technology that identifies printed characters using
    photoelectric devices and computer software.
  • Read: A digital image of a license plate and associated metadata (e.g., date, time, and
    geographic coordinates) captured by the ALPR system.
  • Alert: A visual and/or auditory notice triggered when an ALPR system detects a match
    between a scanned license plate and a plate of interest in the system's database.
  • Hit: A read that matches a license plate on a list of plates related to stolen vehicles,
    wanted individuals, or other investigatory purposes.
  • Hot List: A list of license plate numbers of interest to law enforcement agencies, including,
    but not limited to, stolen cars, vehicles owned by persons of interest, and vehicles
    associated with AMBER Alerts. Hot list information can come from a variety of sources,
    including the National Insurance Crime Bureau and the National Crime Information Center
    (NCIC), national AMBER Alerts and Department of Homeland Security watch lists. The
    Department of Transportation can provide lists of expired registration plates, and agencies
    can interface their own locally compiled hot lists. These lists serve an officer safety
    function as well as an investigatory purpose.
  • Stored ALPR Data: Data, including license plate images, vehicle images, and metadata,
    obtained by the ALPR system and stored for future querying.



Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                               ↑ Contents      Page 69
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 1.4 Procedure
 1.4.1 Management and Oversight
 1.4.1.1 System Oversight: The Chief of Police shall assign a System Administrator who is
 responsible for overseeing the ALPR program, including the installation, maintenance, and
 operation of ALPR units. This individual will also manage system security, data retention, and
 system audits. 1.4.1.2 Data Retention: ALPR data will be retained for 30 days unless needed
 for investigative purposes.

 1.4.2 Authorized User Access
 1.4.2.1 Authorized Personnel: Only authorized department personnel who have received
 training in the use of ALPR systems may access or operate the system. Unauthorized access
 or use of ALPR data is strictly prohibited. 1.4.2.2 Login Requirements: Authorized users must
 log into the ALPR system using their designated credentials. All access will be automatically
 logged, including the date, time, user identity, and purpose of access. 1.4.2.3 Data Access
 Logs: The system will automatically record all queries and access to stored ALPR data,
 including the user, the purpose of the query, and the data retrieved. These logs will be used
 for auditing purposes. 1.4.2.4 Data Confidentiality: ALPR data is confidential and restricted to
 legitimate law enforcement purposes. 1.4.2.5 Background Investigations: Personnel
 authorized to access or use ALPR systems must undergo background investigations, including
 CIB and FBI record checks by fingerprint identification, before being granted access.

 1.4.3 ALPR Usage
 1.4.3.1 Alert Verification: Upon receiving an alert, officers must visually verify that the license
 plate matches the plate read by the ALPR system. Officers must also verify the current status
 of the vehicle through dispatch or a law enforcement database (e.g., NCIC, TIME) before
 taking any enforcement action. 1.4.3.2 Documentation of Actions: Any law enforcement
 actions taken based on an ALPR alert must be documented in a report or citation, including
 the reason for the stop, the verification process, and any enforcement actions taken.
 Standardized statement: "The vehicle was identified via the Automated License Plate Reader
 (ALPR) system. The license plate was visually verified, and the status was confirmed through
 NCIC/TIME before enforcement action was taken." 1.4.3.3 Reporting Successful Uses: Officers
 are required to report all successful uses of the ALPR system (e.g., identification of stolen
 vehicles or wanted persons) to the System Administrator by using the ALPR classification in
 RMS.

 1.4.4 Access to Stored ALPR Data
 1.4.4.1 Querying ALPR Data: Authorized personnel may query stored ALPR data only when
 there is a reasonable belief that the data relates to an active investigation or legitimate law
 enforcement objective. Authorized users shall document the purpose of all ALPR searches. At
 a minimum, documentation for each search shall include: (1) A case number — a Sun Prairie
 or another agency's case number; if none exists yet, a Sun Prairie case number shall be
 generated. (2) A search type selected from the ALPR system's standardized incident
 categories (aligned with crime classifications such as NIBRS). (3) A search reason that briefly
 explains how the search relates to the investigation (e.g., suspect vehicle, victim vehicle, or a
 vehicle known to have been in the area). (4) A lawful objective tied to a legitimate law


Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                               ↑ Contents     Page 70
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 enforcement purpose. ALPR searches shall be conducted and documented in a manner that
 allows the department to verify the validity of each search and demonstrate compliance.
 1.4.4.2 Data Sharing: ALPR data may be shared through the department-approved license
 plate reader system with designated law enforcement agencies. ALPR data shall not be
 shared with federal agencies unless required by court order or expressly approved by the
 Chief of Police.
 1.4.4.3 Dissemination to Non-Law Enforcement Agencies: ALPR data shall only be released to
 law enforcement agencies for legitimate law enforcement purposes. Any request from
 non-law enforcement entities must go through the department's open records process. Any
 other release to non-law enforcement agencies, private individuals, or external organizations
 is strictly prohibited unless required by court order, subpoena, or the chief or their designee.

 1.4.5 Maintenance and Auditing
 The System Administrator shall be responsible for ALPR auditing and audit-related reporting.
 Audits shall assess compliance with this policy and applicable law, including review of system
 access, search activity, documentation of search purpose, and adherence to data retention
 periods. The System Administrator shall compile periodic ALPR usage and audit summary
 reports, which may include the number of reads, alerts, successful deployments, and any
 identified policy violations. The frequency, scope, methodology, and documentation of audits
 shall be set forth in a separate audit procedure document approved by the chief. Audit
 records shall be retained in accordance with department record retention requirements.

 1.4.6 System Updates and Technology Changes
 The department will regularly review its ALPR systems. The System Administrator will be
 responsible for (1) implementing necessary software or hardware updates; (2) ensuring
 updates comply with current legal and regulatory standards; and (3) regularly evaluating the
 system for required modifications to ensure performance and continued compliance with
 privacy laws.

 1.4.7 Privacy Safeguards
 1.4.7.1 Restricted Access: Only authorized personnel with a clear law enforcement purpose
 may access stored ALPR data. All access is automatically logged and subject to regular
 audits. Any employee becoming aware of a possible violation, including unauthorized access,
 use, release and/or dissemination of LPR data, shall refer the matter to his or her supervisor.
 1.4.7.2 Limited Data Usage: ALPR systems are only used for legitimate law enforcement
 objectives, including locating stolen vehicles, identifying wanted persons, and supporting
 criminal investigations. The system shall not be used to, nor is designed to, surveil or track
 individuals or vehicles without reasonable suspicion of a crime. 1.4.7.3 Non-Discriminatory
 Use: ALPR technology shall not be used to monitor individuals or groups based on protected
 characteristics such as race, religion, gender, or political affiliation. 1.4.7.4 Public
 Transparency: The department will provide annual reports summarizing the use of ALPR
 systems, including audits. These reports will be made available to the public in accordance
 with department policies on public records requests.




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                             ↑ Contents     Page 71
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 Flock Audit Procedure
 Flock Audit Procedure — Sun Prairie Police Department (updated April 2026)

 Internal Audit
  • Conducted once a month by the Detective Sergeant.
  • The audit will include any flagged searches in the ARAT queue.
  • Review of search history or irregularities in system use identified by the Audit Review
    Assistance Tool (ARAT).
  • Email any corrective issues to the involved officer, or to seek clarification.
  • Document the search review in the notes section of the ARAT.
  • Flock audits of inappropriate searches will be addressed through an internal investigation.

 External Audit
  • No external audits will be conducted. The information cannot be seen by our agency.
  • We rely on other agencies to conduct audits on the use of the system by their officers.

 Search data that would flag a search
  • Searching the same plate more than 30 days apart
  • Searching entirely outside of owned cameras / solely shared cameras
  • Searching the same plate with multiple case numbers
  • Being the only person searching for one plate and searching for it 10 or more times
  • Narrow search window (less than 96 hours), more than 7 times
 The audit notes will now be stored on Flock's database by entering notes on flagged
 searches.


 Flock Search Screens

 Flock Search Screenshot (March 23, 2026)
 FlockOS search screen (tabs: Search, Alerts, FlockOS, Devices, Evidence):
  • Investigative Purpose: Offense Type* (required); Reason* (required search reason); Case
    Number* (required)
  • Vehicle Details: License Plate; Include Partial Plate; Vehicle Fingerprint (body, make, color,
    etc.); Plate State (Any / Statewide WI)
  • Location & Time: Location — "My Network(s) Selected"; Start 03/23/2026 11:00 AM – End
    03/23/2026 01:00 PM; quick ranges Last 15 Min / 2 Hours / 24 Hours / 48 Hours / 30 Days
  • Map & Devices; Advanced Filters; Reset All; Run Search
 every Flock search requires an offense type, a written reason, and a case number before it
 can run, and searches can use partial plates and "Vehicle Fingerprint" attributes, not just a
 full plate.

 Flock searches photo (product slide)
 Flock product slide titled "Search — Offense Type Dropdown": Flock says it will shift the
 Search Reason open text field to a set of prefilled dropdown reasons, selectable with
 type-ahead, that follow understood Offense Type categories. The list was pulled from NIBRS
 and supplemented with customer feedback and data analysis. Problems Flock says this
 solves: unacceptable search reasons (i.e. "crime"); lack of ability to confidently enforce filters
 on all searches (those with vague search reasons). Stated benefits: more targeted data
 analytics for agencies; improved likelihood for case closures.

 National ALPR Principles (Sept. 23 Senate hearing
 handout)
 Handout from the Sun Prairie Police Department, received at the September 25 meeting.
 Highlighting is from the original. Part of the page break between the two sheets was not
 photographed.
 09/23/26 Senate Judiciary Subcommittee on Crime and Counterterrorism hearing
  • 13 principles released by national law enforcement and public safety organizations
    (International Association of Chiefs of Police, National Sheriff's Organization, National
    District Attorneys Association, National Fusion Center Association, and National High
    Intensity Drug Trafficking Areas, and more)
  • Sun Prairie Police Department currently follows all 13 principles.
  • Written policies. Every law enforcement agency using ALPR systems must adopt and
    enforce clear written policies governing authorized uses, access, documented queries,
    verification, retention, sharing, data security, auditing, and accountability.
  • Permitted uses. ALPR must be used by trained and authorized law enforcement
    personnel only for legitimate law enforcement and public safety purposes.
  • Authorized access. System access must be subject to strong controls, routine auditing,
    and accountability for misuse—and promptly removed when an individual's duties no
    longer require it.
  • Auditing and accountability. Agencies must maintain access logs, conduct routine
    audits, and investigate potential misuse. Policy violations shall result in meaningful
    discipline, up to and including termination, consistent with applicable due process
    requirements. Suspected criminal misuse must be referred to the appropriate authorities
    for investigation and potential prosecution under applicable law.
  • Required training. Personnel with access to ALPR information shall receive initial and
    ongoing training on permitted uses, verification requirements, privacy and security
    protections, and the consequences of misuse.
  • Documented queries. ALPR queries must be tied to an official case number or another
    documented law enforcement or public safety purpose, with query and access logs
    maintained for auditing and accountability.
  • Verification of plate matches. ALPR information must be appropriately verified before
    enforcement action is taken. An alert alone does not identify the driver, establish guilt, or
    replace other investigative work.
  • Confidentiality. ALPR data collected for law enforcement purposes must remain
    appropriately protected from public disclosure. Comparable protections must apply when a
    private company stores or manages the information on behalf of a government agency.
  • Meaningful retention. Retention policies must preserve information needed for
    legitimate investigations while protecting privacy and security. The significance of a
    vehicle may not become apparent until weeks or months after a crime, particularly when a
    victim's report is delayed or an investigation develops over time. Strong access controls,
    documented queries, and auditing are more effective safeguards against misuse than
    destroying potentially important investigative information after an arbitrarily short time


Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                               ↑ Contents       Page 83
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




    period.
  • Responsible data sharing. Law enforcement must be able to responsibly share and
    query ALPR information across jurisdictions because missing people, victims, dangerous
    offenders, and criminal activity do not stop at jurisdictional boundaries. Sharing by
    agencies must be limited to legitimate law enforcement and public safety purposes and
    subject to appropriate controls and oversight. Government agencies must not sell access
    to ALPR data they collect.
  • Law enforcement access to non-governmental ALPR data. The same restrictions,
    safeguards, and accountability requirements must apply to law enforcement's access to
    and use of ALPR data regardless of whether the data was collected by a government
    agency, a commercial provider, or another non-governmental entity.
  • Data security. Law enforcement agencies, technology providers, and other entities that
    store or manage ALPR information must maintain strong security, access, and disclosure
    protections.




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                          ↑ Contents   Page 84

 Product and Services Description
  • Flock Safety Falcon®: An infrastructure-free license plate reader camera that utilizes
    Vehicle Fingerprint® technology to capture vehicular attributes. The Term shall commence
    upon first installation and validation of Flock Hardware.
  • Installation on existing infrastructure: One-time Professional Services engagement.
    Includes site & safety assessment, camera setup & testing, and shipping & handling in
    accordance with the Flock Safety Advanced Implementation Service Brief.
  • Professional Services – Standard Implementation Fee: One-time Professional Services
    engagement. Includes site and safety assessment, camera setup and testing, and shipping
    and handling in accordance with the Flock Safety Standard Implementation Service Brief.
  • Professional Services – Advanced Implementation Fee: One-time Professional Services
    engagement. Includes site & safety assessment, camera setup & testing, and shipping &
    handling in accordance with the Flock Safety Advanced Implementation Service Brief.
 FlockOS Features (Package: Essentials)
  • Community Cameras (Full Access): Access to all privately owned Flock devices within your
    jurisdiction that have been shared with you.
  • Unlimited Users: Unlimited users for FlockOS.
  • State Network (LP Lookup Only): Allows agencies to look up license plates on all cameras
    opted in to the statewide Flock network.
  • Nationwide Network (LP Lookup Only): Allows agencies to look up license plates on all
    cameras opted in to the nationwide Flock network.
  • Direct Share – Surrounding Jurisdiction (Full Access): Access to all Flock devices owned by
    law enforcement that have been directly shared with you. Have ability to search by vehicle
    fingerprint, receive hot list alerts, and view devices on the map.
  • Time & Location Based Search: Search full, partial, and temporary plates by time at
    particular device locations.
  • License Plate Lookup: Look up specific license plate location history captured on Flock
    devices.
  • Vehicle Fingerprint Search: Search footage using Vehicle Fingerprint™ technology. Access
    vehicle type, make, color, license plate state, missing / covered plates, and other unique
    features like bumper stickers, decals, and roof racks.
  • Flock Insights/Analytics page: Reporting tool to help administrators manage their LPR
    program with device performance data, user and network audits, plate read reports, hot
    list alert reports, event logs, and outcome reports.
  • ESRI Based Map Interface: Flock Safety's maps are powered by ESRI, which offers the
    ability for 3D visualization, viewing of floor plans, and layering of external GIS data, such
    as City infrastructure (i.e., public facilities, transit systems, utilities), Boundary mapping
    (i.e., precincts, county lines, beat maps), and Interior floor plans (i.e., hospitals, corporate
    campuses, universities).
  • Real-Time NCIC Alerts on Flock ALPR Cameras: Alert sent when a vehicle entered into the
    NCIC crime database passes by a Flock camera.
  • Unlimited Custom Hot Lists: Ability to add a suspect's license plate to a custom list and get
    alerted when it passes by a Flock camera.

 Master Services Agreement — full text
 Master Services Agreement
 This Master Services Agreement (this "Agreement") is entered into by and between Flock
 Group, Inc. with a place of business at 1170 Howell Mill Road NW Suite 210, Atlanta, GA
 30318 ("Flock") and the entity identified in the signature block ("Customer") (each a "Party,"
 and together, the "Parties") on this the 08 day of September 2023. This Agreement is
 effective on the date of mutual execution ("Effective Date"). Parties will sign an Order Form
 ("Order Form") which will describe the Flock Services to be performed and the period for
 performance, attached hereto as Exhibit A. The Parties agree as follows:
 RECITALS
 WHEREAS, Flock offers a software and hardware situational awareness solution through
 Flock's technology platform that upon detection is capable of capturing audio, video, image,
 and recording data and provide notifications to Customer ("Notifications");
 WHEREAS, Customer desires access to the Flock Services (defined below) on existing devices,
 provided by Customer, or Flock provided Flock Hardware (as defined below) in order to
 create, view, search and archive Footage and receive Notifications, via the Flock Services;
 WHEREAS, Customer shall have access to the Footage in Flock Services. Pursuant to Flock's
 standard Retention Period (defined below) Flock deletes all Footage on a rolling thirty (30)
 day basis, except as otherwise stated on the Order Form. Customer shall be responsible for
 extracting, downloading and archiving Footage from the Flock Services on its own storage
 devices; and
 WHEREAS, Flock desires to provide Customer the Flock Services and any access thereto,
 subject to the terms and conditions of this Agreement, solely for the awareness, prevention,
 and prosecution of crime, bona fide investigations and evidence gathering for law
 enforcement purposes, ("Permitted Purpose").
 AGREEMENT

 NOW, THEREFORE, Flock and Customer agree that this Agreement, and any Order Form,
 purchase orders, statements of work, product addenda, or the like, attached hereto as
 exhibits and incorporated by reference, constitute the complete and exclusive statement of
 the Agreement of the Parties with respect to the subject matter of this Agreement, and
 replace and supersede all prior agreements, term sheets, purchase orders, correspondence,
 oral or written communications and negotiations by and between the Parties.
  • DEFINITIONS
 Certain capitalized terms, not otherwise defined herein, have the meanings set forth or
 cross-referenced in this Section 1.
 1.1 "Anonymized Data" means Customer Data permanently stripped of identifying details and
 any potential personally identifiable information, by commercially available standards which
 irreversibly alters data in such a way that a data subject (i.e., individual person or entity) can
 no longer be identified directly or indirectly.
 1.2 "Authorized End User(s)" means any individual employees, agents, or contractors of
 Customer accessing or using the Services, under the rights granted to Customer pursuant to
 this Agreement.
 1.3 "Customer Data" means the data, media and content provided by Customer through the
 Services. For the avoidance of doubt, the Customer Data will include the Footage.
 1.4. "Customer Hardware" means the third-party camera owned or provided by Customer and
 any other physical elements that interact with the Embedded Software and the Web Interface
 to provide the Services.
 1.5 "Embedded Software" means the Flock proprietary software and/or firmware integrated
 with or installed on the Flock Hardware or Customer Hardware.
 1.6 "Flock Hardware" means the Flock device(s), which may include the pole, clamps, solar
 panel, installation components, and any other physical elements that interact with the
 Embedded Software and the Web Interface, to provide the Flock Services as specifically set
 forth in the applicable product addenda.
 1.7 "Flock IP" means the Services, the Embedded Software, and any intellectual property or
 proprietary information therein or otherwise provided to Customer and/or its Authorized End
 Users. Flock IP does not include Footage (as defined below).
 1.8 "Flock Network End User(s)" means any user of the Flock Services that Customer
 authorizes access to or receives data from, pursuant to the licenses granted herein.
 1.9 "Flock Services" means the provision of Flock's software and hardware situational
 awareness solution, via the Web Interface, for automatic license plate detection, alerts, audio
 detection, searching image records, video and sharing Footage.
 1.10 "Footage" means still images, video, audio and other data captured by the Flock
 Hardware or Customer Hardware in the course of and provided via the Flock Services.
 1.11 "Hotlist(s)" means a digital file containing alphanumeric license plate related
 information pertaining to vehicles of interest, which may include stolen vehicles, stolen
 vehicle license plates, vehicles owned or associated with wanted or missing person(s),
 vehicles suspected of being involved with criminal or terrorist activities, and other legitimate


Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                               ↑ Contents    Page 88
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 law enforcement purposes. Hotlist also includes, but is not limited to, national data (i.e.,
 NCIC) for similar categories, license plates associated with AMBER Alerts or Missing
 Persons/Vulnerable Adult Alerts, and includes manually entered license plate information
 associated with crimes that have occurred in any local jurisdiction.
 1.12 "Installation Services" means the services provided by Flock for installation of Flock
 Services.
 1.13 "Retention Period" means the time period that the Customer Data is stored within the
 cloud storage, as specified in the product addenda.
 1.14 "Vehicle Fingerprint™" means the unique vehicular attributes captured through Services
 such as: type, make, color, state registration, missing/covered plates, bumper stickers,
 decals, roof racks, and bike racks.
 1.15 "Web Interface" means the website(s) or application(s) through which Customer and its
 Authorized End Users can access the Services.
  • SERVICES AND SUPPORT
 2.1 Provision of Access. Flock hereby grants to Customer a non-exclusive, non-transferable
 right to access the features and functions of the Flock Services via the Web Interface during
 the Term, solely for the Authorized End Users. The Footage will be available for Authorized
 End Users to access and download via the Web Interface for the data retention time defined
 on the Order Form ("Retention Period"). Authorized End Users will be required to sign up for
 an account and select a password and username ("User ID"). Customer shall be responsible
 for all acts and omissions of Authorized End Users, and any act or omission by an Authorized
 End User which, including any acts or omissions of authorized End user which would
 constitute a breach of this agreement if undertaken by customer. Customer shall undertake
 reasonable efforts to make all Authorized End Users aware of all applicable provisions of this
 Agreement and shall cause Authorized End Users to comply with such provisions. Flock may
 use the services of one or more third parties to deliver any part of the Flock Services, (such
 as using a third party to host the Web Interface for cloud storage or a cell phone provider for
 wireless cellular coverage).
 2.2 Embedded Software License. Flock grants Customer a limited, non-exclusive,
 non-transferable, non-sublicensable (except to the Authorized End Users), revocable right to
 use the Embedded Software as it pertains to Flock Services, solely as necessary for Customer
 to use the Flock Services.
 2.3 Support Services. Flock shall monitor the Flock Services, and any applicable device
 health, in order to improve performance and functionality. Flock will use commercially
 reasonable efforts to respond to requests for support within seventy-two (72) hours. Flock will
 provide Customer with reasonable technical and on-site support and maintenance services
 in-person, via phone or by email at support@flocksafety.com (such services collectively
 referred to as "Support Services").
 2.4 Upgrades to Platform. Flock may make any upgrades to system or platform that it deems
 necessary or useful to (i) maintain or enhance the quality or delivery of Flock's products or
 services to its agencies, the competitive strength of, or market for, Flock's products or
 services, such platform or system's cost efficiency or performance, or (ii) to comply with

5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 applicable law. Parties understand that such upgrades are necessary from time to time and
 will not diminish the quality of the services or materially change any terms or conditions
 within this Agreement.

 2.5 Service Interruption. Services may be interrupted in the event that: (a) Flock's provision
 of the Services to Customer or any Authorized End User is prohibited by applicable law; (b)
 any third-party services required for Services are interrupted; (c) if Flock reasonably believe
 Services are being used for malicious, unlawful, or otherwise unauthorized use; (d) there is a
 threat or attack on any of the Flock IP by a third party; or (e) scheduled or emergency
 maintenance ("Service Interruption"). Flock will make commercially reasonable efforts to
 provide written notice of any Service Interruption to Customer, to provide updates, and to
 resume providing access to Flock Services as soon as reasonably possible after the event
 giving rise to the Service Interruption is cured. Flock will have no liability for any damage,
 liabilities, losses (including any loss of data or profits), or any other consequences that
 Customer or any Authorized End User may incur as a result of a Service Interruption. To the
 extent that the Service Interruption is not caused by Customer's direct actions or by the
 actions of parties associated with the Customer, the time will be tolled by the duration of the
 Service Interruption (for any continuous suspension lasting at least one full day). For
 example, in the event of a Service Interruption lasting five (5) continuous days, Customer will
 receive a credit for five (5) free days at the end of the Term.
 2.6 Service Suspension. Flock may temporarily suspend Customer's and any Authorized End
 User's access to any portion or all of the Flock IP or Flock Service if (a) there is a threat or
 attack on any of the Flock IP by Customer; (b) Customer's or any Authorized End User's use of
 the Flock IP disrupts or poses a security risk to the Flock IP or any other customer or vendor
 of Flock; (c) Customer or any Authorized End User is/are using the Flock IP for fraudulent or

 Compensation insurance as required by applicable State law for all Flock employees.

 or Excess Liability insurance written on an occurrence basis with minimum limits of Ten
 Million Dollars ($10,000,000) per occurrence and Ten Million Dollars ($10,000,000) in the

 Five Million Dollars ($5,000,000) per occurrence and Five Million Dollars ($5,000,000) in the
 aggregate; (iv) Commercial Automobile Liability insurance with a minimum combined single
 limit of One Million Dollars ($1,000,000) per occurrence for bodily injury, death, and property
 coverage, including owned and non-owned and hired automobile coverage; and



Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                               ↑ Contents     Page 99

 (v) Cyber Liability insurance written on an occurrence basis with minimum limits of Five
 Million Dollars ($5,000,000).

 Axon ALPR Brief
 Axon ALPR program brief (vendor document, current as of September 2026).
 Axon ALPR is built around agency control, system safeguards, and an audit record the agency
 can review and share to meet the expectations of the communities it serves.

 What Sets Us Apart
  • Purpose-built security: For nearly 2 decades, Axon has securely managed agencies' most
    sensitive data, from body camera footage to public safety records.
  • Agencies own and control their data: Agencies own their data outright, including how it's
    accessed and exported. Data stays with each agency, with access granted only at their
    explicit direction.
  • Built responsibly, from the start: Axon engages with stakeholders from the earliest stages
    of product design, including its Ethics & Equity Advisory Council, public safety
    professionals, academic researchers, and community voices.
  • Federal access: There is no automatic, standing or built-in federal access to agency ALPR
    data through Axon.
  • Lawful requests: If Axon receives a lawful request, it directs the request to the agency as
    the data owner.
  • Access control: Recipient agencies' access to data they receive does not include the ability
    to extend that access to others. The originating agency retains full control over who can
    access its data.
  • Data security: Axon does not access agency data, other than for support requests, which
    are time-boxed, logged, and revoked once resolved.
  • Agency ownership: Agencies own all hardware, from poles, to permits, to cameras.
    Switching providers or adding other vendors' equipment to that infrastructure is entirely
    the agency's call.

 Key Safeguards
  • Plates, not people: Axon ALPR captures license plates, not identities — no facial
    recognition, no biometric matching, no profiles of people or owners. Each detection logs a
    plate image, a vehicle context image, basic attributes (color, make, type), and
    time/location, checked only against hotlists an agency has authorized.
  • Access permissions and search controls: Separate permissions control who can search
    detections and hits, manage hotlists and administer the system. Every ALPR search
    requires a stated reason and an offense category the agency has authorized, and each
    agency can choose whether to require a case number.
  • Agency-configured access & retention: Every data search is subject to the originating
    agency's authorized offense categories, and recipients see only detections matching their
    own hotlists. Agencies set retention independently for detections and hits, and data is
    automatically removed when the retention period expires.
  • Audit logs and reporting: Agencies can generate and export audit reports covering ALPR
    activity, data use, access controlled relationships and hotlist changes.



Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                             ↑ Contents   Page 101
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




  • Public transparency: Agencies can publish aggregate ALPR information, including hit-alert
    counts and current data partnerships. The portal does not publish plate numbers, images,
    individual records or camera locations.
  • CJIS compliance & data security: Axon Cloud Services meet the FBI's CJIS Security Policy
    across all 13 policy areas, with tamper-proof audit trails on every evidence access, and
    controls independently validated each year via SOC 2 Type 2 with CJIS validation.

 Ongoing Enhancements
  • Additional access controls: requiring a full plate or case number before a search proceeds,
    plus other field-level requirements agencies can define.
  • Automated anomaly flagging and reporting: flags unusual search patterns, such as the
    same plate searched repeatedly or under different case numbers, for administrative
    review, and adds a recurring audit digest.
  • Outcome reporting: records outcomes from ALPR searches and hotlist follow-ups into
    standardized audit and transparency reporting.
 Axon says it is committed to continuing to invest in additional safeguards, and that its
 platform helps operationalize compliance with federal, state, and local policy by automating
 retention schedules and generating audit-ready reports.
 Additional resources: ALPR Council and Community Engagement Toolkit; ALPR@axon.com;
 https://trust.axon.com/ ; https://www.axon.com/getstarted/alpr-council-toolkit
 This roadmap represents Axon's current plans and is subject to change. © 2026 Axon
 Enterprise, Inc.

 SPPD ALPR Frequently Asked Questions (October
 2026)
 Sent by Chief Kevin Warych on October 16, 2026, in reply to Zackary's Oct. 6 follow-up
 questions.

 Automated License Plate Reader (ALPR) Technology: Frequently
 Asked Questions
 The Sun Prairie Police Department (SNPD) is committed to using technology responsibly to
 enhance public safety while respecting the privacy and rights of our community members.
 As the department transitions from Flock Safety to Axon automated license plate reader
 (ALPR) technology, we recognize that residents may have questions about how the system
 works, what information is collected, who can access it, and how that information is
 protected.
 The following frequently asked questions explain the technology, its intended use, and the
 safeguards in place to promote transparency, accountability, and responsible data
 management.

 Understanding the Technology
 What is an Automated License Plate Reader (ALPR)?
 An Automated License Plate Reader (ALPR) is a camera-based technology that captures
 images of vehicles and their license plates as they pass a specific location.
 ALPR technology provides law enforcement with an additional investigative tool to help
 identify vehicles associated with criminal activity, locate missing persons, and support other
 legitimate public safety investigations.
 The cameras capture information at a specific point in time. They do not provide continuous,
 real-time tracking of vehicles or individuals.

 What information do ALPR cameras collect?
 The Axon ALPR system captures:
  • An image of the vehicle's license plate.
  • A broader image of the vehicle and its surroundings.
  • Basic visible vehicle characteristics, such as color, make, and vehicle type.
  • The date, time, and location where the vehicle was photographed.
 Because the cameras capture images of the surrounding area, photographs may incidentally
 include nearby people, signs, buildings, or other objects.
 However, the system does not use facial recognition technology or analyze images to identify
 pedestrians or vehicle occupants.




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                              ↑ Contents   Page 105
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 Can the cameras identify drivers, passengers, or pedestrians?
 No. The ALPR system is designed to identify license plates and capture basic vehicle
 information—not to identify individuals.
 Although a person may incidentally appear in a photograph, the system does not perform
 facial recognition or use those images to identify drivers, passengers, or pedestrians.
 The system also does not automatically retrieve registered vehicle owner information or
 create profiles of vehicle owners.

 Can ALPR cameras be used to monitor speeding or issue traffic citations?
 No. Neither the department's current Flock cameras nor the planned Axon ALPR cameras
 measure vehicle speed.
 The Axon ALPR system does not currently have speed-detection capabilities, and there are no
 current or near-term plans to introduce them.

 Why does the Sun Prairie Police Department use ALPR technology?
 ALPR technology is one of several investigative tools used by the Sun Prairie Police
 Department to help protect the community.
 It can assist officers with:
  • Identifying vehicles associated with criminal investigations.
  • Locating stolen vehicles.
  • Supporting investigations involving missing or endangered persons.
  • Developing investigative leads.
  • Identifying vehicles connected to serious incidents.
  • Helping investigators use available resources more efficiently.
 ALPR technology does not replace traditional police work or independent investigative
 judgment. It provides additional information that may help officers investigate incidents and
 respond to public safety concerns.

 Has ALPR technology made a difference in Sun Prairie?
 The Sun Prairie Police Department has experienced meaningful investigative benefits from its
 use of ALPR technology.
 The system has assisted officers in identifying vehicles of interest, developing investigative
 leads, and supporting criminal investigations.
 The department has shared examples of successful ALPR-assisted investigations with the
 Public Safety Committee and Common Council.
 While individual investigative successes do not independently establish that ALPR technology
 reduces overall crime rates, they demonstrate how the technology can support local law
 enforcement efforts.




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                             ↑ Contents    Page 106
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 Why is the department transitioning to Axon?
 The transition to Axon provides an opportunity for the department to continue using ALPR
 technology while strengthening its approach to data management, information sharing, and
 accountability.
 Key considerations include:
  • Greater control over which agencies can access Sun Prairie's information.
  • Formal data-sharing agreements.
  • Restrictions on federal agency access.
  • Detailed audit records of system activity.
  • Continued City ownership of ALPR data.
  • An opportunity to establish a shorter data retention period.
 The department's goal is to maintain a useful investigative tool while implementing
 safeguards that reflect the community's expectations regarding privacy, transparency, and
 responsible use.

 Data Ownership, Storage & Retention
 Who owns the information collected by ALPR cameras?
 The City of Sun Prairie owns and controls the information collected by its ALPR cameras.
 This includes license plate images, vehicle photographs, associated data, and information
 generated through the system.
 Axon does not acquire ownership rights to the City's ALPR data.

 How long is ALPR information stored?
 The Sun Prairie Police Department currently retains ALPR information for 30 days.
 The final retention period for the Axon system has not yet been established. As part of the
 transition to Axon, the department is evaluating a shorter retention period that balances
 investigative needs with community privacy considerations.
 Once the established retention period expires, the system automatically deletes the
 information in accordance with its retention settings.

 What happens to the data if the City ends its contract with Axon?
 If the City terminates its agreement with Axon, the Police Department will no longer be able
 to use the technology or access its data through the system.
 The City retains ownership of its information and has 90 days following termination to
 retrieve its data. After that period, the data is deleted. The City can also export audit and
 activity reports for its records.

 Who pays to store the information?
 The City pays a fee for each ALPR camera, which includes the cost of data storage.
 The system automatically deletes information after the established retention period. This
 prevents the indefinite accumulation of data and helps maintain predictable storage



Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                              ↑ Contents    Page 107
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 requirements.
 The City's data is not sold to third parties to offset storage costs.

 Can Axon sell ALPR information to insurance companies or other businesses?
 No. Axon cannot sell the City's ALPR data to insurance companies, private businesses, or
 other third parties.
 The City owns and controls the information collected by its cameras.
 The information is not collected for commercial purposes and is not available for sale.

 Data Access, Security & Accountability
 Who can access Sun Prairie's ALPR information?
 Access is restricted to authorized Sun Prairie Police Department employees, including sworn
 personnel and dispatchers, whose responsibilities require use of the system.
 Access may also be granted to specifically approved state or local law enforcement agencies
 through a formal agreement with the City.
 Each user receives access based on their assigned role and responsibilities.

 Can Axon employees access the City's ALPR information?
 Axon employees do not have unrestricted or ongoing access to the City's ALPR data.
 Access may be temporarily authorized when the Police Department requests technical
 assistance. Any such access must be:
  • Specifically approved by the department.
  • Limited to the purpose of the support request.
  • Restricted to the time necessary to complete that request.
  • Logged and available for audit.

 How is the information protected from unauthorized access?
 The ALPR system incorporates multiple security safeguards, including:
  • Role-Based Access: Users can access only the features and information authorized for their
    assigned responsibilities.
  • Multi-Factor Authentication (MFA): Users must complete an additional identity verification
    step when accessing the system.
  • CJIS Compliance: The system meets applicable Criminal Justice Information Services (CJIS)
    security requirements used to protect sensitive law enforcement information.
  • Audit Logs: System activity is recorded so the department can review how the technology
    is being used.
 These safeguards help protect information from unauthorized access and support
 accountability.




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                            ↑ Contents     Page 108
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 Who is authorized to search the ALPR database?
 Authorized Police Department employees, including sworn officers and dispatchers, may
 conduct searches for legitimate law enforcement purposes.
 Access to the system does not mean an employee has unlimited authority to search the
 database.
 All searches must comply with department policies and established restrictions.

 What is required to conduct an ALPR search?
 Every ALPR search must be connected to an authorized law enforcement purpose.
 Before conducting a search, users must enter:
 1. An authorized offense category.
 2. A specific reason for the search.
 The department determines which offense categories are permitted.
 If a user selects an offense category that has not been authorized by the department, the
 system will not return ALPR data.
 The offense category and stated reason are recorded in the system's audit history.

 Are all searches logged?
 Yes. Every search is logged.
 Audit records allow the department to review system activity, including who conducted a
 search and the reason provided. This includes searches performed by users with
 administrative privileges.

 Can employees or administrators delete their search history?
 No. Users cannot delete or modify their search history.
 System administrators can review audit records but cannot alter or erase searches from the
 audit log.
 This safeguard helps ensure accountability and supports the investigation of potential
 misuse.

 What prevents an employee from using the system for personal reasons?
 The department has established safeguards to help prevent and detect unauthorized use.
 Every search requires a stated law enforcement purpose and is associated with an
 identifiable user.
 Because searches are logged and cannot be deleted by users or administrators, the
 department can review activity and investigate potential misuse.
 Using ALPR information for personal reasons—such as monitoring a former partner,
 acquaintance, or other individual without a legitimate law enforcement purpose—would be an
 unauthorized use of the system.




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                           ↑ Contents     Page 109
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 Information Sharing with Other Agencies
 Does the Sun Prairie Police Department share ALPR information with other law
 enforcement agencies?
 Yes, but sharing will be limited, controlled, and subject to formal agreements.
 Under the planned Axon system, the department intends to establish a more restrictive
 approach to information sharing than was available through its previous Flock system.
 Information will be shared only with approved state and local law enforcement agencies that
 have entered into a formal Memorandum of Understanding (MOU) with the City.
 There will be no blanket sharing of ALPR information.

 Will federal agencies have access to Sun Prairie's ALPR database?
 No. The department will not share ALPR database access with federal agencies.
 Axon's ALPR sharing system does not provide built-in or automatic federal agency access.
 The Sun Prairie Police Department will also prohibit ALPR data-sharing agreements with
 agencies participating in the federal 287(g) immigration enforcement program.
 This restriction is intended to ensure that access remains consistent with the City's
 established public safety priorities and data-sharing requirements.

 Can another agency share Sun Prairie's ALPR information with additional
 agencies?
 No. The Axon system is designed to prevent agencies from redistributing ALPR data that they
 do not own.
 The system uses a method known as federated sharing, which allows an approved agency to
 access authorized information without receiving ownership of a separate copy of the
 database.
 An agency can share only ALPR data that it owns.

 Can another agency use Sun Prairie's ALPR information to investigate crimes
 outside Wisconsin?
 Potentially, yes, provided the search meets the requirements established in the formal
 data-sharing agreement.
 Each participating agency must agree to the authorized uses of the information.
 The City will not permit its ALPR data to be used for purposes that violate the agreement or
 fall outside the approved law enforcement objectives.
 If an agency violates the terms of its agreement, the City will discontinue sharing ALPR
 information with that agency.




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                             ↑ Contents     Page 110
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 What happens if another agency requests ALPR information for an
 unauthorized purpose?
 The department will not authorize database access for purposes outside its approved policies
 and data-sharing agreements.
 Formal agreements will identify the expectations and limitations governing access.
 Requests made through legal processes will be reviewed by the City and its legal counsel, as
 appropriate.
 If Axon receives a legal request for the City's data, the company directs the request to the
 City as the data owner and discloses information only when legally required.

 Privacy & Community Concerns
 Are ALPR cameras used to continuously track people?
 No. ALPR cameras do not provide a continuous live feed of an individual's location or follow
 vehicles as they travel.
 Instead, each camera captures a vehicle and license plate when the vehicle passes that
 specific location.
 During an authorized investigation, law enforcement personnel may search previously
 collected records to determine when and where a particular license plate was photographed.
 These searches are limited to information available within the department's established
 retention period.

 Could ALPR cameras be used to monitor visits to medical facilities or other
 sensitive locations?
 The department recognizes concerns about the potential misuse of ALPR technology,
 particularly regarding visits to medical facilities, reproductive health providers, or other
 sensitive locations.
 The ALPR system does not identify why a person visits a particular location or monitor an
 individual's activities.
 However, a license plate search may reveal that a vehicle was photographed at a particular
 location and time.
 For this reason, access to ALPR information is restricted to authorized law enforcement
 purposes, and searches are subject to audit and oversight.
 The department's data-sharing agreements will also establish restrictions intended to prevent
 other agencies from using Sun Prairie's information for unauthorized purposes.

 What safeguards prevent ALPR information from being used for activities that
 are not crimes in Wisconsin?
 The department will establish formal data-sharing agreements defining how its ALPR
 information may be used.
 These agreements will identify authorized purposes and prohibit uses that fall outside those
 requirements.


Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                              ↑ Contents    Page 111
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 For example, the department does not intend to permit another agency to use Sun Prairie's
 ALPR database to investigate conduct that is lawful in Wisconsin solely because it is
 prohibited in another jurisdiction.
 Agencies that violate the terms of their agreements will lose access to Sun Prairie's ALPR
 information.

 Does the system create profiles of residents or their travel habits?
 The Axon ALPR system does not automatically create behavioral profiles of individuals or
 registered vehicle owners.
 It collects individual vehicle detections at specific locations and times.
 Authorized law enforcement personnel may search historical detections associated with a
 particular license plate for an approved investigative purpose.
 Those searches are logged and subject to department oversight.

 Our Commitment to the Community
 The Sun Prairie Police Department recognizes that public trust is essential to effective law
 enforcement.
 We are committed to using ALPR technology in a manner that supports public safety while
 protecting individual privacy and promoting accountability.
 Our guiding principles are straightforward:
  • Public Safety: Use technology to support legitimate law enforcement investigations and
    protect the community.
  • Privacy: Limit the collection, retention, access, and sharing of information.
  • Accountability: Maintain auditable records of system activity and establish clear
    expectations for authorized use.
  • Transparency: Provide residents with understandable information about how the
    technology works and the safeguards governing its use.
 We welcome community questions and remain committed to evaluating our practices as
 technology, legal requirements, and community expectations evolve.
 — Kevin Warych, Chief of Police, Sun Prairie Police Department




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                              ↑ Contents   Page 112

 Problems Found Elsewhere: Audits and Misuse
 Documented cases from other places, with sources. None of these involve Sun Prairie. They
 show why written safeguards matter.
  • Illinois, August 2025. Secretary of State Alexi Giannoulias said Flock Safety broke a
    2023 state law by letting U.S. Customs and Border Protection access Illinois plate data
    through a pilot program. Flock disagreed with the legal conclusion, said it "clearly
    communicated poorly," and paused its federal pilot programs. Evanston deactivated its
    Flock cameras. ([NPR Illinois](https://www.nprillinois.org/illinois/2025-08-27/hundreds-of-p
    olice-departments-use-camera-company-accused-of-breaking-state-law))
  • Out-of-state search, 2025. The same reporting says out-of-state police used Flock
    earlier in 2025 to search for a woman who had recently had an abortion, which prompted
    the Illinois audit. (Same source.)
  • San Francisco, June 2026. An SFPD compliance audit found 299 improper searches of its
    Flock network over about a year, run through a regional intelligence center on behalf of
    federal and out-of-state agencies. The Chief cut off that center's access. Flock said no
    out-of-state or federal agency had direct access. ([KTVU](https://www.ktvu.com/news/sfpd
    -audit-shows-flock-cameras-accessed-behalf-federal-out-of-state-agencies))
 What these have in common: the problems were found by audits of search logs. That's
 why monthly audits and public results are on Zackary's list of terms to put in writing.




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                             ↑ Contents   Page 115
5. LICENSE PLATE READER CAMERAS (FLOCK → AXON)




 Plate Reader Glossary
  • ALPR — Automated license plate reader: a camera that photographs passing vehicles and
    turns the plate into searchable text.
  • Hit — An alert when a scanned plate matches a plate on a hotlist.
  • Hotlist — A list of plates police are looking for, such as stolen vehicles or wanted persons.
  • Retention — How long scans are kept before they're deleted. Flock's 2023 contract: 30
    days. Axon proposal: a 14-day trial (Chief, Oct 27).
  • Audit log — A record of every search: who searched, when, and why.
  • Audit — A review of the audit log to catch improper searches. The Chief has said a
    Detective Sergeant would audit monthly.
  • Data sharing — Letting other agencies search your cameras' data. Usually set by an
    agreement (MOU).
  • MOU — Memorandum of understanding: a written agreement between agencies.
  • 287(g) — A federal program that lets local police help with immigration enforcement.
    SPPD's FAQ says Sun Prairie data won't be shared with 287(g) agencies.
  • NCIC — The FBI's National Crime Information Center, a source for hotlists.
  • Livestreaming — Watching camera feeds live. The Chief said Axon won't livestream (Oct
    27).
  • Outpost — Axon's solar plate reader camera, the model in the draft estimate.
  • Falcon — Flock's plate reader camera.
  • Policy F-18 — SPPD's written plate reader policy.

 E-141 · Concerns Regarding the Use of Flock Safety Cameras in Sun Prairie
 From            Zackary Mark <zackarymark160@gmail.com>
 To              sschmidt@cityofsunprairie.com, tveith@cityofsunprairie.com, Alder Rex Owens
                 <rowens@cityofsunprairie.com>, Mayor <mayor@cityofsunprairie.com>, Chief Kevin Warych

 Date            Jun 21, 2026, 4:50 PM
 Topics          Plate readers · Safety & mental health · City & Mayor
 Thread          Same conversation: E-142, E-143, E-144, E-145, E-146, E-147, E-148, E-149, E-150, E-151,
                 E-152, E-153
   Dear Sun Prairie City Council Members, Public Safety Committee Members, and Chief Warych,
   I am writing to express my concerns regarding the use and potential expansion of Flock Safety
   cameras within the City of Sun Prairie.
   First, I want to acknowledge that I understand the purpose of these cameras. I recognize that the
   Sun Prairie Police Department controls access to the system and that the cameras are intended to
   assist with criminal investigations, locate stolen vehicles, and improve public safety. I appreciate
   the efforts of our law enforcement officers to keep our community safe and understand why some
   community members support the technology.
   However, I remain concerned about the broader implications of these cameras, particularly
   regarding privacy, transparency, community trust, and immigration-related enforcement.
   While Sun Prairie may not currently use Flock cameras for immigration enforcement purposes,
   there is documented evidence from across the country showing that Flock data has been accessed
   and used in immigration-related investigations through partnerships between local law
   enforcement agencies and federal authorities. Reports have shown that immigration officials have
   requested assistance from local agencies with access to Flock systems, raising concerns about how
   data collected for local public safety purposes could potentially be used beyond its original intent.
   Even if these concerns do not currently apply to Sun Prairie, the existence of these documented
   cases creates understandable fear and uncertainty among immigrant families and community
   members. Residents deserve confidence that data collected within our city will not be used,
   directly or indirectly, to support immigration enforcement activities.



Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                                       ↑ Contents     Page 117
6. MORE EMAILS: CITY, POLICE, DISTRICT & STATE (JUNE–NOV. 2026)




   I also recognize that Sun Prairie currently has a relatively small number of Flock cameras, with
   reports indicating approximately six cameras throughout the city. While this number may seem
   limited, my concern is not simply the number of cameras but the precedent they establish. Even a
   small surveillance network can collect significant amounts of data on residents and visitors over
   time. As these systems become more integrated into law enforcement operations, it is important to
   consider their long-term impact on privacy, public trust, and the possibility of future expansion.
   I am also concerned about transparency and community trust. Many residents are told that these
   cameras are being used solely for public safety purposes, yet questions remain regarding data
   retention, information sharing, oversight, and future uses of the technology. When residents are
   uncertain about how their information may be accessed, shared, or utilized, trust in local
   government and law enforcement can be weakened.
   Public safety and civil liberties should not be viewed as opposing goals. A truly safe community is
   one where residents feel protected from crime while also feeling confident that their privacy and
   rights are being respected. Before any future expansion of Flock cameras is considered, I
   encourage the City Council to establish clear safeguards, strengthen transparency measures, limit
   data sharing, and provide strong assurances that information collected through these systems will
   not be used for immigration enforcement purposes.
   I would also appreciate clarification regarding whether Sun Prairie has policies in place that prohibit
   Flock camera data from being shared for immigration enforcement purposes, either directly or
   indirectly through requests from federal agencies. If such policies exist, I believe they should be
   made clear and readily accessible to the public. If they do not exist, I encourage the City to
   consider adopting them in order to strengthen community trust and provide residents with greater
   confidence in how their data is protected.
   As a Sun Prairie resident, student at Sun Prairie West High School, and student advocate who has
   worked with local leaders on issues affecting our community, I care deeply about maintaining both
   public safety and public trust. I believe transparency, accountability, and community engagement
   are essential when implementing technologies that have the potential to impact the privacy and
   rights of residents.
   I respectfully urge the City to carefully consider these concerns and engage the community in an
   open discussion about the future of surveillance technology in Sun Prairie.
   Thank you for your time and consideration. I look forward to your response regarding the City's
   policies and safeguards related to the use of Flock camera data.
   Sincerely,
   Zackary Mark Sun Prairie Resident Sun Prairie West High School (608) 940 7356
   zackarymark160@gmail.com

 E-143 · RE: Concerns Regarding the Use of Flock Safety Cameras in Sun Prairie
 From            Chief Kevin Warych <kwarych@cityofsunprairie.com>
 To              Zackary Mark <zackarymark160@gmail.com>, sschmidt@cityofsunprairie.com,
                 tveith@cityofsunprairie.com, Alder Rex Owens <rowens@cityofsunprairie.com>, Mayor

 Date            Jun 22, 2026, 2:44 PM
 Attachmen       F-18 Automatic License Plate Readers.pdf, Flock searches photo.jpg, Flock Search Screenshot
 ts              2026-03-23.png
 Topics          Plate readers · Budget · Safety & mental health · City & Mayor
 Thread          Same conversation: E-141, E-142, E-144, E-145, E-146, E-147, E-148, E-149, E-150, E-151,
                 E-152, E-153
   Mr. Mark,
   Thank you for your email and for taking the time to share your concerns regarding license plate
   reader (LPR) technology. To avoid a walking quorum, I blind copied to city council and public safety
   as they were referenced in your initial email. First and foremost, I want you to know that I am
   always open to meeting in person and would be happy to sit down with you to discuss these issues
   further. Email can be helpful for exchanging information, but it does not always provide the full
   context needed for topics as nuanced and important as this one. Please consider this a standing
   invitation, not only for you, but for anyone in our community who would like to discuss license plate
   readers or any other public safety issue.
   To answer your initial question, the Sun Prairie Police Department currently has a total of nine
   license plate readers. Six of these cameras are funded directly by the department, while three
   others were obtained through donations. Currently, the department has no intention of expanding



Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                                       ↑ Contents    Page 119
6. MORE EMAILS: CITY, POLICE, DISTRICT & STATE (JUNE–NOV. 2026)




   the number of cameras, nor are there any plans under development to do so. I would like to
   address your concerns by category to ensure I provide a complete response.
   Privacy and Constitutional Considerations I appreciate that privacy is one of the most significant
   concerns surrounding license plate reader technology. Frankly, I believe these concerns are
   reasonable and deserve thoughtful discussion. Public trust is not built by dismissing concerns, but
   rather by listening to them, being transparent, and explaining how decisions are made.
   From a legal perspective, courts have consistently recognized that there is generally no reasonable
   expectation of privacy in a license plate that is displayed in plain view on a vehicle traveling on
   public roadways. In Katz v. United States (1967), the United States Supreme Court established the
   "reasonable expectation of privacy" test and held that what an individual knowingly exposes to the
   public is generally not protected by the Fourth Amendment.
   In United States v. Knotts (1983), the Court further held that "a person traveling in an automobile
   on public thoroughfares has no reasonable expectation of privacy in his movements from one place
   to another," as those movements are voluntarily exposed to public observation.
   Similarly, in New York v. Class (1986), the Court found that individuals do not have a reasonable
   expectation of privacy in information that is required by law to be displayed on a vehicle and
   visible to the public, such as a Vehicle Identification Number (VIN). This same rationale has been
   applied by courts to license plates displayed on public roads.
   More recently, courts reviewing Automated License Plate Reader technology have generally
   concluded that the capture of a license plate and vehicle image at a point in time, visible to anyone
   traveling on a public roadway, does not by itself constitute an unreasonable search under the
   Fourth Amendment.
   I recognize there is an ongoing national discussion regarding how emerging technologies should be
   used and what safeguards should exist. I know people disagree about these issues, and I respect
   those differing viewpoints. My goal is not to persuade you one way or the other, but rather to
   provide the facts and explain how the Sun Prairie Police Department approaches the use of this
   technology.
   It is also worth noting that many businesses, convenience stores, and shopping centers utilize
   security cameras that capture considerably more information than a license plate and vehicle.
   Nonetheless, I understand that concerns regarding government use of technology are different and
   deserve additional scrutiny and discussion.
   Transparency The Sun Prairie Police Department prioritizes transparency in everything we do. This
   commitment is reflected in our regular news releases, weekly police blotters, public safety
   messaging, and our willingness to discuss our policies openly with the community.
   With regard to license plate readers specifically, we strive to be as transparent as possible while
   complying with Wisconsin's Open Records laws. These laws are important because they balance an
   individual's privacy interests with the public's right to know.
   We have been open about incidents in which license plate readers have assisted in solving crimes,
   locating suspects involved in violent crimes, recovering stolen vehicles, and otherwise improving
   public safety in Sun Prairie If there were ever an incident that jeopardized community trust, (if the
   system were used improperly), we have accountability measures in place to investigate and
   address that conduct.
   I understand why people may be skeptical, particularly given examples from other parts of the
   country where officers and or specific license plate vendors were not transparent and damaged
   public trust. That is something I take very seriously because public trust is foundational to effective
   policing. Without it, we cannot do our job.



Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                                      ↑ Contents    Page 120
6. MORE EMAILS: CITY, POLICE, DISTRICT & STATE (JUNE–NOV. 2026)




   Community Trust and Police Legitimacy Everything we do at the Sun Prairie Police Department
   revolves around community trust. Every call for service, traffic stop, investigation, and community
   interaction depends on maintaining that trust. Our residents deserve nothing less. For me, the
   more important question is not whether technology exists, but whether it is used in a manner that
   is lawful, transparent, and deserving of the public's trust.
   The department's support for license plate reader technology is not based on a vendor, a product,
   or a business relationship. Our support is based on the public safety outcomes we have seen and
   the belief that, when appropriately governed, this technology can assist in locating stolen vehicles,
   identifying suspects involved in violent crimes, locating missing persons, and providing
   investigative leads that help protect our community. Using technology to our benefit is more
   important than ever given our tight budgets.
   Equally important are the safeguards surrounding its use. The department has an established
   policy governing license plate reader technology, which I have attached per your request. Officers
   are trained in the lawful and ethical use of the technology, and every search conducted within the
   system is auditable. The technology shall only be used for lawful law enforcement purposes. If it
   were ever used improperly, I can assure you it would be addressed through our accountability
   processes.
   Police legitimacy is not achieved simply because something is legal. It is earned through
   transparency, accountability, procedural justice, and maintaining the confidence of the people we
   serve. I recognize that some members of the community remain skeptical of this technology, and I
   respect that perspective. My responsibility as Chief is to ensure that if we use a tool, we do so in a
   way that is constitutional, transparent, and consistent with the values of Character, Competence,
   and Leadership that guide our department.
   Ultimately, I support license plate reader technology because I believe it can enhance public safety
   while operating within clearly defined legal and ethical boundaries. At the same time, I remain
   committed to ongoing dialogue with our community to ensure that trust and legitimacy remain at
   the center of everything we do.
   Immigration-Related Enforcement The Sun Prairie Police Department does not have authority to
   enforce federal immigration law. I have issued directives to ensure our officers act within their
   constitutional authority and clearly understand the limitations of our role. More importantly, we do
   not share our license plate reader data with Federal authorities nor for immigration enforcement
   purposes, and the system has been configured to prevent its use for immigration enforcement by
   federal authorities. Please see attached screenshots regarding this.
   I understand there are concerns that agencies with whom data is shared could potentially misuse
   information. While I cannot speak for every agency, the license plate reader system requires
   specific investigative criteria and documented reasons for searches. This creates an audit trail and
   provides accountability should questions ever arise regarding how data was accessed or used.
   This aspect of the technology is admittedly difficult to explain fully over email, which is one reason
   I would welcome an in-person discussion. I have attached several screenshots that may help
   provide additional perspective on how the system functions, as well as the department's policy
   regarding license plate readers.
   My goal is not to convince you to agree with the technology, but rather to ensure you have
   accurate information and understand the safeguards we have in place. I hope this email addresses
   your questions and concerns. If not, I would be more than happy to continue the conversation in
   person. Thank you again for reaching out and for your interest in public safety in our community.
   Respectfully,

 From            Chief Kevin Warych <kwarych@cityofsunprairie.com>

 Cc              sschmidt@cityofsunprairie.com, tveith@cityofsunprairie.com, Alder Rex Owens
