 ts
 Topics          Plate readers · Budget · Safety & mental health
 Thread          Same conversation: E-141, E-142, E-143, E-144, E-145, E-147, E-148, E-149, E-150, E-151,
                 E-152, E-153
   Good Morning Zach
   I hope this information helps. I want to reiterate the standing invite to meet on this. I am happy to
   take time and provide context this. I separated your email into your questions and addressed each
   one. I hope you find this helpful
   First, you mentioned that the system is configured to prevent its use for immigration enforcement
   by federal authorities. Could you explain how that restriction works in practice?
   Currently, the Sun Prairie Police Department utilizes Automated License Plate Reader (ALPR)
   technology through the Flock Safety platform. Within the system, the department has intentionally
   configured search settings to prohibit searches related to immigration status and reproductive
   health matters. As a result, department personnel are not authorized to use the system for those
   purposes. This expectation is reinforced through departmental directives and policy. Additionally,
   the Sun Prairie Police Department does not share its ALPR data with federal agencies.



Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                                       ↑ Contents    Page 123
6. MORE EMAILS: CITY, POLICE, DISTRICT & STATE (JUNE–NOV. 2026)




   Is the restriction applied only to federal agencies, or are there also safeguards regarding agencies
   that may cooperate with federal immigration enforcement efforts?
   The restrictions on prohibited search purposes apply to all users of the system, regardless of
   agency affiliation. When conducting a search, officers are required to select an offense category
   from a predefined list, which is intended to ensure that searches are narrowly tailored and
   consistent with authorized law enforcement purposes. These are the screen shots I included in the
   previous email.
   In the interest of transparency, it is important to acknowledge that no technology can completely
   eliminate the possibility of misuse. An individual could potentially select an incorrect offense
   category when conducting a search. As with many law enforcement systems, the department relies
   on a combination of policy, supervision, and auditing to promote compliance and accountability.
   The department remains committed to monitoring system use and addressing any concerns that
   may arise to maintain community trust.
   Second, which law enforcement agencies currently have access to Sun Prairie's license plate
   reader data, and under what circumstances is data shared with outside agencies?
   The Sun Prairie Police Department shares ALPR data with law enforcement agencies in neighboring
   states and Indiana, consistent with legal guidance. This network includes approximately 200
   participating agencies.
   The purpose of this data sharing is to assist in legitimate criminal investigations and public safety
   efforts. Importantly, the Sun Prairie Police Department does not share its ALPR data with federal
   agencies. As far as data sharing, we share if the agency requests and are also using Flock.
   Third, how long is license plate reader data retained before it is automatically deleted, and has that
   retention period changed since the cameras were first implemented?
   The department's ALPR policy provides specific guidance regarding data retention. Currently, ALPR
   data is retained for 30 days before being automatically deleted. This retention period has remained
   unchanged since the system was initially implemented.
   I am also curious about the auditing process. How often are audits conducted, who reviews them,
   and have there been any instances in which the system was found to have been accessed or used
   improperly?
   The department has established a formal audit procedure for ALPR use, which is outlined in the
   attached procedure. This procedure was developed in 2025 and updated again in April in response
   to community feedback and reflects the department's commitment to transparency,
   accountability, and responsible use of technology.
   Later in 2025, Flock Safety updated its platform to require users to select an offense category
   before conducting a search, providing an additional layer of accountability and documentation.
   Prior to that enhancement, the department reviewed searches and followed up with agencies when
   search justifications were incomplete or did not appear to align with approved criteria.
   To date, the department has not identified intentional misuse of the system by Sun Prairie
   personnel. Nonetheless, audits remain an important safeguard, and the department is committed
   to continuously reviewing system use, strengthening oversight, and maintaining the community's
   trust through transparent practices and responsible stewardship of this technology.
   Finally, has the department evaluated the effectiveness of the cameras? For example, are there
   statistics regarding stolen vehicles recovered, crimes solved, missing persons located, or other
   measurable public safety outcomes associated with the technology?




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                                     ↑ Contents     Page 124
6. MORE EMAILS: CITY, POLICE, DISTRICT & STATE (JUNE–NOV. 2026)




   Yes, we have maintained a list of success stories. It is just the successes of ALPR and how it has
   helped public safety in Sun Prairie. It does not contain statistics. This list has been shared with
   Council President Owens. Out of respect to the council, I want to make sure they have had a
   chance to review this before I share with you. I will be sharing this again in the budget meetings in
   July.
   I hope this helps. If you would like to meet, please let me know.
   Kevin Warych

   Prairie." The report is not affiliated with the City or SPPD, and I tried to distinguish between

   researching and organizing the report.
   Since our conversation on September 25th, I've continued looking into the transition from Flock to
   Axon Outpost, and I have a few additional questions I was hoping you could help clarify before the
   Axon proposal moves forward.
   First, regarding the Axon Outpost system itself:
    • How many Axon Outpost cameras is SPPD currently planning to purchase or deploy, and are the
      proposed locations already determined?
    • Will Sun Prairie be using Outpost strictly for automated license plate recognition, or will other
      capabilities such as live video streaming and vehicle-attribute recognition be enabled?
    • Is Axon Fusus included as part of the proposed contract? If so, what exactly would SPPD be able
      to connect to or access through Fusus?
    • If vehicle-attribute recognition is enabled, would officers be able to search for vehicles based on
      characteristics such as color, make, model, roof racks, bumper stickers, or other attributes
      without already knowing a license plate?
    • Would Policy F-18 automatically apply to those additional capabilities, or would SPPD need to
      update the policy before using them?
   I also have some questions about data and the transition away from Flock:
    • What will happen to Sun Prairie's historical Flock data once the current cameras are removed?
      Will it be transferred to the city, exported, deleted, or remain with Flock?
    • Will the city continue to have access to historical Flock data after the Flock contracts expire?
    • What will the exact retention period be under the Axon contract for plate reads, vehicle
      attributes, video, and audit logs?
    • Who will have access to the new Axon data, including other law-enforcement agencies, federal
      agencies, Axon employees, or subcontractors?
    • Will all searches and accesses be logged in a way that SPPD can audit?

    • Will the Axon contract prohibit Axon from using Sun Prairie data, including de-identified data, for

 E-198 · RE: Thank you for the FAQ, and a few follow-up questions
 From            Chief Kevin Warych <kwarych@cityofsunprairie.com>
 To              Zackary Mark <zackarymark160@gmail.com>
 Date            Oct 27, 2026, 8:18 PM
 Topics          Plate readers · Budget
 Thread          Same conversation: E-197, E-199
   Hi Zach. I am currently at a budget work session. Please see my response below
   Kevin
   The Chief answered inline under each question (shown in red in the original email). His answers,
   copied as written:
   1. Fusus and livestreaming: There will be no livestreaming. We did not pay for that nor do I
   want that.
   2. The old Flock data: After 30 days, this data will be deleted. The one question I have to follow
   up on is the audit logs, as those are kept for transparency efforts.
   3. Retention: I/PD decides this. The retention period for Axon will be 14 days as a trial period for
   three months. In those three months, I will ask my staff to determine if any investigations are being
   affected, if no investigations are affected, it will remain at 14. If this is problematic it will be moved
   to 21.
   4. Audits: Detective Sgt reviews them. And this will be done monthly. However, knowing the Sgt,
   the audit may be done more frequently.
   5. Policy F-18: Policies are always up for evaluation. Though we do not write policies specific
   vendor. We right it to the technology. I will work to get that policy on our website if approved.
   6. The contract: Since the city owns the data we control who we share with. The master
   agreement is subject to open record. If ALPR is approved, the legal department has to review the
   contract and send to Axon. Once the agreement is signed then the appropriate next step is to
   develop an MOU on the sharing of data. I will make this a public facing document.
   7. Timing: This is at committee now. The city has budget work sessions tonight (currently at the
   meeting), next week Tuesday the 3rd 10th, with the final approval of the budget on Nov 17th.

 What the Chief's Oct 27 answers add, as department statements (not contract terms):


Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                                        ↑ Contents     Page 144
6. MORE EMAILS: CITY, POLICE, DISTRICT & STATE (JUNE–NOV. 2026)




  • No livestreaming with Axon.
  • Stored Flock data is deleted after 30 days; whether Flock audit logs are kept is still being
    followed up on.
  • Axon retention starts at 14 days as a three-month trial, set by the Chief/SPPD, moving to
    21 days if investigations are affected.
  • Searches audited monthly by a Detective Sergeant.
  • The ALPR policy (F-18) is written to the technology, not the vendor; the Chief will work to
    post it on the SPPD website if approved.
  • The master agreement is subject to open records. After signing, a data-sharing MOU will
    be developed and made public.
  • Timing: at committee now, tied to city budget work sessions; final budget approval Nov 17
    (the Nov 3 session was corrected: no meeting that day).

 E-206 · Letter to the editor: Sun Prairie's plate readers
 From            Zackary Mark
 To              Editor, Hometown News Group (Sun Prairie Star)
 Date            Nov 9, 2026, 8:00 AM
 Topics          Plate readers · Media
   Sun Prairie is considering switching its license plate readers from Flock to Axon. Whatever you
   think of the technology, one fact deserves attention: so far, the rules are mostly promises.
   The Chief has said there would be no livestreaming, that data would be kept 14 days during a trial,
   and that a Detective Sergeant would review searches monthly. I appreciate that. But I have not
   seen a contract, and a promise only binds anyone once it is written down.
   Other cities show why it matters. Cedar Rapids, Iowa switched from Flock to Axon under a
   164-page contract. Henrico County, Virginia ended its program and removed more than 120
   cameras. Bloomington, Indiana banned plate readers.
   My request is simple. Put the retention period, the livestreaming rule and the audit schedule in
   writing, and publish the data-sharing agreement before the council votes. Then residents can judge
   the actual rules, not just the intentions.

   Hello,
   I'm Zack Mark, a student at SPWHS. I run an independent, unofficial site about Sun Prairie's license
   plate camera program: sunprairiealpr.netlify.app. I'm not affiliated with the City or the police
   department, though I've met Chief Warych several times.
   Here is what's public: 9 cameras now, Flock keeps data 30 days, the department plans to move to
   Axon in May 2027 with 14 days proposed for a trial, and Axon's draft estimate is $235,389 over 10
   years (non-binding, expires Nov. 30). Budget approval is planned for Nov. 17, though I can't
   confirm Axon is part of that vote.
   What isn't public yet is the data-sharing agreement and written audit rules. The Chief says he's
   open to a quarterly report.
   If this is useful for a story, every fact on my site lists its source, and the archive has the source
   documents. I'm glad to be a point of contact, and I won't ask you to take a side. I used AI to help
   build the site and say so on it.
   Thanks, Zack Mark sunprairiealpr@gmail.com

10. GET INVOLVED




 How to Speak Up
 How any student or family member can do what's in this record.

 Email a district leader
 1. Find the right person. The Who's who page and the district website list roles.
 2. Keep it short: who you are, what you're asking, and one clear question.
 3. Ask for something specific: an answer, a meeting, or a document.
 4. If there's no reply after a week, follow up once, politely.

 Speak at a Board of Education meeting
 1. Check the posted agenda on BoardDocs (go.boarddocs.com/wi/spasd/Board.nsf).
 2. Sign up for public comment the way the agenda describes, usually before the meeting
    starts.
 3. Plan for about three minutes. Say your name, your school, your point and one ask.
 4. Bring a written copy to hand in.

 Talk to the Board's student representatives
 They serve two-year terms, from the end of sophomore year through senior year. Recruiting
 starts in December and applications open in mid-January. You can reach the current reps any
 time.

 Ask the city for public records
 Wisconsin's public records law lets anyone ask for government records. Police records go
 through the city's online records portal; other city records go to the City Clerk. Describe
 exactly what you want and the date range.

 Comment on a city decision
 Email the Mayor and the committee, speak during public comment in Council Chambers at
 300 E. Main St., or ask your alder where they stand. The City Clerk's office can tell you how to
 sign up.




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                              ↑ Contents   Page 163
10. GET INVOLVED




 Email Templates
 Fill in the brackets and keep it short.

 Asking a district leader a question
    Subject: Question about [topic]

    Hi [Name],

    My name is [your name], and I'm a student at [school]. I've been following [topic] and want to
    make sure I understand it correctly.

    [One or two specific questions.]

    Thank you for your time. I'd be glad to talk if that's easier.

    [Your name]


 Asking for a meeting
    Subject: Could we meet about [topic]?

    Hi [Name],

    I'd like 15–20 minutes to talk about [topic]. I'm free [two or three times]. I can meet in person
    or online.

    Thank you,

    [Your name]


 Following up
    Subject: Following up: [original subject]

    Hi [Name],

    I wanted to follow up on my email from [date] about [topic]. I know you're busy. If someone
    else is the right person, I'd appreciate a pointer.

    Thank you,

    [Your name]


 Public records request (Wisconsin)
    To the records custodian:

    Under Wisconsin's public records law, I request copies of [describe the records] for the period
    [start date] to [end date]. Please send them electronically if possible. If you deny any part of
    this request, please explain why in writing. If any part is redacted, please release the rest.

10. GET INVOLVED




 FAQ About These Projects
 Is this official? No. SPASD Student Voice and Sun Prairie ALPR are independent, student-led
 projects. They are not run by the Sun Prairie Area School District, the City of Sun Prairie or the
 police department.
 Who writes it? Zackary Mark. Officials' words are quoted from their own emails, and every
 email is shown with its sender and date.
 Who pays for it? No one. There is no funding and no sponsor.
 Why are other students' names missing? Only Zackary's name appears. Other students
 are described by their role ("a work group member") to protect their privacy.
 Is anything left out? Yes: raw survey responses, raw meeting recordings and transcripts,
 personal matters and unrelated class projects. Nothing was left out to change what an official
 said.
 Can I join? Yes. DM @spasdstudentvoice or @sunprairiealpr, or email
 studentvoicesp@gmail.com (Student Voice) or sunprairiealpr@gmail.com (plate readers). No
 experience needed.
 What if something here is wrong? Email the project address above. Corrections are listed
 in the Corrections log with the date they were made.




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                               ↑ Contents   Page 166
10. GET INVOLVED




 How a Decision Gets Made
 Simplified. Exact steps vary; check the posted agenda for each meeting.

 How a school district decision gets made
 1. Staff or a work group drafts it. Example: the weighted grades work group, with
    students in it.
 2. A Board committee or the Committee of the Whole discusses it. Example: Nov 2
    (Teaching & Learning Committee) and Nov 9 (Committee of the Whole).
 3. It goes on a regular Board meeting agenda for a vote. Public comment usually
    comes near the start.
 4. The Board votes. A majority passes it.
 5. District staff carry it out. That's when "you said, we did" gets tested.
 Where students can step in: join the work group, email Board members before steps 2
 and 3, speak during public comment, and check that step 5 happens.

 How a city budget decision gets made
 1. Departments request funding. Example: SPPD's plan to move from Flock to Axon.
 2. Staff and the Mayor put together a proposed budget.
 3. Committees and budget work sessions review it. Example: Oct 27 and Nov 10 work
    sessions.
 4. The Common Council holds a public hearing and adopts the budget. Planned for
    Nov 17.
 5. Contracts and policies follow. The Axon contract, Policy F-18 and data-sharing
    agreements come after the money is approved.
 Where residents can step in: email alders before step 3, speak at the budget hearing in
 step 4, and ask for contract terms to be public before step 5.




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                           ↑ Contents   Page 167
10. GET INVOLVED




 Lessons for Student Advocates
 Practical things that worked this year, from the emails in this record.
  • Send the email. Most changes here started with one short, polite email to the right
    person.
  • Ask one clear question. "Will these six terms be in the contract before the vote?" gets a
    better answer than a long list of concerns.
  • Follow up once, kindly. Many replies came after a second email.
  • Put it in writing. After a meeting, send a recap of what was agreed. That's how the
    newsletter commitment became a written one.
  • Find the right door. When someone says "that's not my call," ask who decides and go
    there.
  • Say what you're not. Stating that the projects are independent heads off confusion.
  • Keep receipts. Save every email and note. This archive exists because of that habit.
  • Protect other students. Use roles, not names, in anything public.
  • Correct mistakes in public. A corrections log builds more trust than pretending to be
    perfect.

 Goals for 2027 (draft)

 SPASD Student Voice
  • Get the District-Wide Student Council approved as a school-sponsored pilot with a staff
    partner.
  • Launch the district-wide student survey in December and publish the results.
  • Keep the monthly "You said, we did" report going all year.
  • Recruit at least one student contact at every middle and high school.
 Sun Prairie ALPR
  • See the retention period, audit rules and data-sharing agreement in writing.
  • Publish the first quarterly transparency numbers if SPPD releases them.
  • Get the records requests answered and posted.
  • Have the site legally reviewed for accuracy.
 Both
  • Plan the handoff so both projects continue after Zackary.

    "Making a Difference Through Student Voice," added; follow-ups to the Board President,
    Dr. Morgan, the Chief and the Mayor.
  • Nov 7 — Letter to the Board of Education with questions about the superintendent's
    license.
  • Nov 8–9 — Zackary sent East an SP30 slide and poster; public records request and letter
    to the editor on plate readers; Chief open to a quarterly transparency report.
  • Nov 9 — Weighted grades plan goes to the Board's Committee of the Whole.
  • Nov 10 — Central Heights points the council to its Student Council; caregiver survey
    analysis released for the public record.
  • Coming up — Nov 12 Patrick Marsh planning meeting, Nov 17 weighted grades follow-up
    and city budget approval, late November weighted grades vote.

APPENDIX




 Independence, Funding & AI Use

 Independence and funding
 This record and both projects are independent and student-led. They are not affiliated with,
 approved by, or speaking for the Sun Prairie Area School District, the City of Sun Prairie, or
 the Sun Prairie Police Department. No one pays for this work, and no organization sponsors it.

 How AI tools were used
 Zackary uses AI tools to help draft, organize and format writing, including parts of this record.
 Facts, quotes and numbers are checked against the original emails and documents, and
 officials' emails are copied as written. If you find an error, it's Zackary's to fix, and corrections
 are logged at the back.




Zackary Mark — Volume 2: Sun Prairie ALPR · v3.1                                 ↑ Contents    Page 175
APPENDIX




 Handoff Plan
 How these projects keep going when Zackary graduates or steps back. This is a plan, not a
 commitment from anyone named.
 What exists
  • Two Instagram accounts (@spasdstudentvoice, @sunprairiealpr) and two websites on free
    hosting.
  • This record, rebuilt from the same sources each update.
  • Open threads with the district, the city and SPPD (see Still Unanswered and the Promises
    Tracker).
 Handoff steps
 1. Find two successors per project before spring, ideally from different schools and
    grades, so one graduation doesn't end the work.
 2. Shared access. Move both Instagram accounts and website logins to a shared project
    email, not a personal one. Turn on two-step sign-in.
 3. Introductions by email. Introduce successors in writing to Dr. Reed's office, Dr. Mould,
    Ms. Bevenue, Chief Warych and the principals who replied, so the threads continue.
 4. The council. Ask the district to name a staff sponsor so the District-Wide Student Council
    outlasts any one student.
 5. The record. Keep publishing "You said, we did" monthly and a full update each semester.
    Keep the rules: no student names, emails as written, corrections logged.
 6. Overlap. Run one full cycle together (one Board meeting and one city vote) before
    stepping away.

 Resumen en español (Spanish Summary)
 Resumen en español de este registro. Actualizado el 10 de noviembre de 2026.
 Zackary Mark es un estudiante defensor en Sun Prairie West High School. Dirige dos
 proyectos independientes: SPASD Student Voice (desde enero de 2026), que lleva las
 opiniones de los estudiantes a los líderes del Distrito Escolar del Área de Sun Prairie, y Sun
 Prairie ALPR (desde julio de 2026), un informe comunitario en lenguaje sencillo sobre las
 cámaras lectoras de placas de la policía.
 Lo que cambió gracias a los estudiantes
  • Los boletines del distrito llegarán a todos los estudiantes (confirmado por escrito el 21 de
    octubre).
  • El distrito confirmó por escrito el 1 de octubre que las protecciones LGBTQIA+ no cambian.
  • Estudiantes participaron en las entrevistas de director y conocieron a los finalistas para
    superintendente; se reinició el Senado Estudiantil en Sun Prairie West.
  • Estudiantes ayudaron a escribir el plan de calificaciones ponderadas, que llegó a la Junta el
    9 de noviembre. Se espera la votación final a fines de noviembre.
  • El superintendente, Dr. Reed, apoyó la idea de un Consejo Estudiantil de todo el distrito.
    Patrick Marsh ya se unió.
  • El jefe de policía puso compromisos por escrito: una prueba de 14 días para guardar datos,
    sin transmisión en vivo, auditorías mensuales y un acuerdo público para compartir datos.
 Encuesta a familias (octubre de 2026): 531 respuestas; el 70% apoya las calificaciones
 ponderadas.
 Cámaras Axon propuestas: $235,388.97 en 10 años (estimado preliminar, no es un
 contrato). El Concejo Municipal vota el presupuesto el 17 de noviembre.
 Cómo participar: escriba a @spasdstudentvoice en Instagram o a
 studentvoicesp@gmail.com (sobre las cámaras: sunprairiealpr@gmail.com). Puede hablar en
 las reuniones públicas de la Junta Escolar y del Concejo Municipal.

ABOUT ME




 About Me

 IN THIS PART
 Contact                                                                                   p. 185
 Thank You                                                                                 p. 185
 What Changed in Each Update                                                               p. 186
 I'm Zackary Mark, a student at Sun Prairie West High School.
 This work started in January 2026 with an email to the Board of Education about student
 safety and trust, and it kept growing from there: the Act 95 and weighted grades work group,
 the student voice survey and public reports, meetings with district leaders about the budget,
 mental health and student voice, and a community report on Sun Prairie's license plate
 reader cameras.
 I'm not part of a formal organization. I do this on my own and alongside other students
 because I think young people should be able to understand the decisions that affect them
 and ask questions of the people who make them.
 Outside of advocacy, I teach martial arts at Kicks Unlimited in Sun Prairie, speak English and
 Spanish, and compete on the SPWHS Forensics team. I plan to become a social studies
 teacher.
 I run two projects: SPASD Student Voice (spasdstudentvoice.netlify.app,
 @spasdstudentvoice) for student voice in the district, and Sun Prairie ALPR
 (sunprairiealpr.netlify.app, @sunprairiealpr), an independent look at the city's license plate
 reader cameras.

 too.
